## Cognitive Science / Educational Psychology

### From: [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundation|developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]]

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational review establishing schema theory as the dominant framework for reading comprehension research. Demonstrates comprehensively the relationship between prior knowledge and comprehension quality. Central framework for Section 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational empirical demonstration that memory and comprehension are schema-driven and constructive, not reproductive. His cross-cultural folk tale experiments remain compelling evidence for schema-driven comprehension distortion.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The landmark study establishing the self-explanation effect. Documented that students who spontaneously self-explained during learning dramatically outperformed those who did not, launching a productive research program on elaborative learning strategies. Central to Section 5.3.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The most authoritative systematic review of the evidence base for learning strategies, classifying 10 techniques by utility based on literature review. Establishes retrieval practice and elaborative interrogation as high-utility strategies; re-reading and highlighting as low-utility. Essential practical reference for Section 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The definitive statement of the metacognition framework introducing the distinction between metacognitive knowledge and metacognitive experiences, and identifying metacognitive monitoring as a critical self-regulatory process. Foundational for Section 3.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Kintsch's landmark theoretical paper articulating the Construction-Integration model and establishing the theoretical distinction between textbase and situation model construction. The foundational text for the entire multi-level comprehension framework discussed in Section 1.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Establishes the monitoring-control framework that provides the formal computational model for metacognitive processes during reading and learning. Essential theoretical infrastructure for Section 3.2.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The founding paper of Reciprocal Teaching, documenting the design rationale and initial research evidence for the four-strategy collaborative reading protocol. The most frequently cited paper in reading comprehension instruction research. Essential for Section 7.2.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Landmark experimental demonstration of the testing effect showing that retrieval practice dramatically outperforms re-study for long-term retention, even when initial learning is equated. Central evidence for the strategy hierarchy in Section 5.4.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Establishes the three-phase self-regulated learning model (forethought, performance, self-reflection) that frames the metacognitive architecture of self-directed academic reading in Section 7.4.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational review establishing schema theory as the dominant framework for reading comprehension research. Demonstrates comprehensively the relationship between prior knowledge and comprehension quality. Central framework for Section 6.

> [!cite] Reference

## Cognitive Science / Personal Knowledge Management

### From: [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05|pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]]

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational paper establishing the multicomponent working memory model. This report's entire analysis depends on the architectural distinction between the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive as separate, limited-capacity systems. Essential for establishing why offloading strategies target specific modality-bound subsystems rather than "memory" monolithically. **Read first.**

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational paper for the Extended Mind Thesis and the Parity Principle. Introduces the coupling conditions that constitute the quality standards for genuine extended cognition. The Inga/Otto thought experiment is the original instantiation of the PKM-as-extended-mind argument. Essential for the theoretical grounding of Sections 3 and 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The paper that introduced "cognitive offloading" as a unified construct with its tripartite taxonomy (motor, perceptual, computational) and the ease-of-offload framework. Provides empirical grounding for the observation that offloading is regulated by cost-benefit calculation, not pure information type. Directly supports Section 2.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational paper for Cognitive Load Theory. Introduces the distinction between formats that impose different cognitive loads on working memory during problem-solving. Provides the theoretical infrastructure for the Extraneous-First and Generative Investment principles developed in Section 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Recalibrated capacity estimates from Miller's 7±2 to approximately 4±1 units, and argued that these units are episodic chunks defined by context, not fixed-size storage slots. Crucial for understanding the realistic limitations of working memory and why offloading strategies are cognitively valuable even for relatively short information strings.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Introduced the concept of epistemic action — modifying the environment to simplify internal computation — through empirical study of expert Tetris players who rotated pieces physically rather than mentally. Directly supports the Retrieval-as-Computation principle and the characterisation of the PKB as an epistemic action infrastructure.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Demonstrated that knowing information is externally accessible before encoding reduces retention — the "Google Effect." Directly relevant to the Substitution Trap discussion in Section 2 and the Scaffold-Dependence Risk in Section 7. Shows that accessibility shapes internal encoding investment, supporting the Generative Investment Principle.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Extended empirical study of distributed cognition in ship navigation, showing that cognitive processes genuinely extend across human and technological components in complex sociotechnical systems. Provides empirical grounding for the extended mind framework at the systems level and for the Distributed Cognition framework invoked in the Far Transfer sections.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Overview of developments in Cognitive Load Theory, including the interaction between load types and learner expertise (expertise reversal effect). Relevant to the boundary conditions discussion: what counts as extraneous load depends on the practitioner's expertise level, not only on the instructional design.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Reported superior conceptual test performance from handwritten versus laptop notes, arguing that typing enables verbatim transcription that bypasses reformulation. Directly relevant to the Generative Investment Principle and the Conceptual Tensions section on note-taking medium. Should be read alongside Morehead et al. (2019) for replication context.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Overview of desirable difficulties — conditions that slow initial learning but improve long-term retention and transfer (spacing, testing, interleaving, generation). Directly supports both the Generative Investment Principle and the Scaffold-Dependence Risk discussion. The generation effect, spacing effect, and testing effect are all relevant to PKM review design.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Proposed "long-term working memory" — the use of long-term memory retrieval structures to extend effective working memory capacity in skilled practitioners. Provides theoretical infrastructure for understanding how expert PKM practitioners may develop rapidly accessible knowledge structures that effectively reduce working memory overhead through [[long-term-memory]] reorganisation.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational paper establishing the multicomponent working memory model. This report's entire analysis depends on the architectural distinction between the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive as separate, limited-capacity systems. Essential for establishing why offloading strategies target specific modality-bound subsystems rather than "memory" monolithically. **Read first.**

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational paper for the Extended Mind Thesis and the Parity Principle. Introduces the coupling conditions that constitute the quality standards for genuine extended cognition. The Inga/Otto thought experiment is the original instantiation of the PKM-as-extended-mind argument. Essential for the theoretical grounding of Sections 3 and 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The paper that introduced "cognitive offloading" as a unified construct with its tripartite taxonomy (motor, perceptual, computational) and the ease-of-offload framework. Provides empirical grounding for the observation that offloading is regulated by cost-benefit calculation, not pure information type. Directly supports Section 2.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* The foundational paper for Cognitive Load Theory. Introduces the distinction between formats that impose different cognitive loads on working memory during problem-solving. Provides the theoretical infrastructure for the Extraneous-First and Generative Investment principles developed in Section 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Recalibrated capacity estimates from Miller's 7±2 to approximately 4±1 units, and argued that these units are episodic chunks defined by context, not fixed-size storage slots. Crucial for understanding the realistic limitations of working memory and why offloading strategies are cognitively valuable even for relatively short information strings.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Introduced the concept of epistemic action — modifying the environment to simplify internal computation — through empirical study of expert Tetris players who rotated pieces physically rather than mentally. Directly supports the Retrieval-as-Computation principle and the characterisation of the PKB as an epistemic action infrastructure.

> [!cite] Reference
> *Annotation:* Demonstrated that knowing information is externally accessible before encoding reduces retention — the "Google Effect." ### From: [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05|schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text for constructive memory and schema theory. Bartlett's "War of the Ghosts" experiments demonstrated systematic schema-driven distortion in recall, establishing that memory reconstruction is shaped by the observer's existing knowledge structures. Directly relevant to this report's account of why schemas are processing engines rather than storage containers. Sections II-V most directly relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical computational specification of schemas as data structures with slots, defaults, and constraints. Rumelhart's formalization provides the specific structural description this report uses to specify schema-fidelity properties for PKB design. Required background reading for Sections 2 and 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that introduced the Parity Principle and the extended mind thesis. Despite its brevity (12 pages), this paper generated more philosophical commentary than any other article in philosophy of mind in the subsequent two decades. The Otto/Inga thought experiment and the formulation of the Parity Principle are directly drawn on in Section 5. Accessible even without philosophy background.

> [!cite] Reference
> The founding paper of Cognitive Load Theory. Establishes the relationship between working memory constraints and schema construction during learning, providing the theoretical basis for the offloading-vs-externalization distinction developed in Section 4. Somewhat technical; Van Merriënboer & Sweller (2005) provides a more accessible overview.

> [!cite] Reference
> A landmark experimental demonstration of the testing/retrieval practice effect. Shows that taking memory tests produces better long-term retention than restudying, even when the initial test performance is lower. Foundational support for the claim in Section 3 that retrieval practice strengthens schemas more effectively than passive review.

> [!cite] Reference
> A comprehensive review of the cognitive offloading literature. Defines the phenomenon, reviews empirical evidence, and distinguishes types and consequences of offloading. Provides the theoretical grounding for distinguishing offloading from schema externalization in Section 1 and Section 4.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original two-component working memory model paper. Establishes the multicomponent architecture that CLT subsequently built on. Directly relevant to Section 4's account of working memory constraints and the schema-working memory interface.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical spreading activation model of semantic memory. Provides the specific computational mechanism — activation propagating through weighted associative links — that the PKB knowledge graph structure is proposed to externally analogize. Relevant to Sections 3 and 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates that consistent access to searchable databases reduces content memory while improving source memory (memory for where information can be found). Provides empirical grounding for the substitution-risk discussion in Section 8 and the transactive memory shift from content to location memory.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational self-explanation effect paper. Demonstrates that students who generate explanations for solution steps during studying learn significantly more than those who do not. The self-explanation effect is the primary mechanistic evidence for the outside-in direction of the Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text for constructive memory and schema theory. ## Cognitive Science / Reading Comprehension

### From: [[developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08|developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]]

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The foundational paper introducing the propositional theory of text comprehension that became the CI model. Introduces the textbase concept and the analysis of macro-structure construction. This report's theoretical core.
> **Recommended section:** Entire paper for foundations; Section 2 on macro-structure most directly relevant to academic reading.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The paper introducing the Construction-Integration model's two-phase cycle. Essential reading for understanding the spreading activation and constraint satisfaction mechanisms discussed throughout this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The original reciprocal teaching study, reporting both the theoretical rationale and the first experimental evidence. Effect sizes were large; this paper launched three decades of reciprocal teaching research and replication.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The definitive reference on vocabulary acquisition for reading comprehension, including the coverage threshold research establishing 98% as the functional minimum for adequate comprehension. Directly informs the vocabulary section of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The primary reference for the Tier 1/2/3 vocabulary framework used throughout this report. Provides both theoretical framework and practical instructional guidance for academic vocabulary development.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The foundational study demonstrating large effects of self-explanation during text reading on comprehension and learning. The mechanism is identified as forced situation model construction through inference generation. Directly informs Section 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The seminal theoretical account of cumulative advantage and disadvantage in reading development. The Matthew Effect metaphor (from Matthew 25:29) is introduced here. This paper's influence on reading research has been enormous and continues to shape intervention priorities.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** Short theoretical paper introducing the metacognition construct in its mature form. Defines metacognitive knowledge and metacognitive regulation in terms that remain standard. Critical background for this report's metacognition sections.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** Introduces the five-dimensional Event-Indexing Model of situation model construction. Compact paper with large empirical and theoretical impact. Informs Section 5 on inference generation and situation model dimensions.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** Accessible and elegant account of why background knowledge is the most powerful lever in reading comprehension development. Argues that reading comprehension "skill" is substantially a knowledge issue. Widely assigned in teacher education; directly relevant to the knowledge-building debate (Section 7).

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The key theoretical chapter developing modern schema theory as a computational-cognitive framework. Defines schemas, explains their activation and inheritance properties, and articulates the schema theory account of reading comprehension. Essential background for Section 4.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** Comprehensive synthesis of Ann Brown's metacognitive monitoring research in the context of reading comprehension. Key reference for the comprehension monitoring and self-regulation sections.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The constructionist theory paper distinguishing coherence from elaborative inferences and characterising the conditions under which each is generated. Informs Section 5's inference generation analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation:** The foundational paper introducing the propositional theory of text comprehension that became the CI model. Introduces the textbase concept and the analysis of macro-structure construction. This report's theoretical core.

## Cognitive Psychology

### From: [[habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28|habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive contemporary review of habit formation science. Covers the dual-system architecture, context-action encoding, the habit-goal interface, and the conditions for habit formation and change. Phases III and IV draw extensively on this review. Essential reading for anyone seeking a comprehensive scholarly treatment.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational paper establishing the basal ganglia's role in chunking behavioral sequences. Phase IV's mechanism analysis is grounded in this work. Recommended for readers who want the primary neuroscience rather than secondary synthesis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original systematic treatment of habit in psychology. More prescient about mechanism than later commentators typically acknowledge, and still worth reading directly for its treatment of the practical implications of habit formation for human freedom and development.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most methodologically careful real-world habit formation timeline study. Key findings: 66-day median, 18–254-day range, asymptotic growth function, robustness to missed instances. Phase III's evidence review draws directly on this study. Limitations: self-report measurement, selected sample, simple behaviors.

> [!cite] Reference
> Human fMRI study providing direct evidence for the dorsolateral striatum's role in habit formation, extending Graybiel's animal research to human participants. Phase III and IV draw on this for the human neurobiological evidence.

> [!cite] Reference
> Wood's accessible synthesis of her research program. Provides the context-specificity principle, the transitional period finding, and the environment design framework in their most developed form. Phase V's practical recommendations are consistent with Wood's framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> Authoritative review of dual-process theory, clarifying the Type 1/Type 2 distinction and responding to critiques. Phase II's analytical framework draws on this for the dual-system architecture.

> [!cite] Reference
> Popular synthesis with some empirical grounding. Clear's four laws and identity-based habits framework are practically valuable but mechanistically incomplete; Phase II explicitly notes this limitation. Useful as a practical implementation guide for the mechanisms this report analyzes.

### From: [[metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21|metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational study establishing that readers can apply comprehension standards when explicitly instructed but rarely apply them spontaneously. Key evidence for the monitoring-regulation decoupling problem. Supports Phase III and the central analytical reframe.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal review establishing comprehension monitoring as a core metacognitive skill in reading. Distinguished monitoring from other reading skills and provided the framework that subsequent decades of research built on. Cited in Phase III and throughout.

> [!cite] Reference
> Classic study demonstrating schema-dependent comprehension — participants who read the "washing clothes" passage without a title showed severely impaired comprehension and recall. Used in Phase III to establish that comprehension failures are often invisible to the reader.

> [!cite] Reference
> Established the self-explanation effect: readers who generate self-explanations during reading show dramatically superior comprehension and transfer compared to passive readers. Key evidence for the externalization principle in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the metacognitive knowledge/regulation/experience trichotomy used throughout this analysis. Particularly important for the concept of metacognitive experience as the trigger layer for regulation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive treatment of metacognitive failures in reading. Identified the "compile to understand" illusion, documented the evidence on adult readers' poor monitoring, and provided the fix-up strategy taxonomy used in Phase IV. Core reference for the fluency trap mechanism.

> [!cite] Reference
> The authoritative account of the construction-integration model of text comprehension, including the textbase/situation model distinction used throughout this analysis. Provides the cognitive architecture within which comprehension monitoring must be understood.

> [!cite] Reference
> Established the automaticity account of skilled reading — the theoretical foundation for the fluency trap mechanism. The argument that decoding automatization frees resources for comprehension is here complicated by the insight that those resources are not automatically redirected to monitoring.

> [!cite] Reference
> The monitoring-control model of metacognition used as the primary theoretical framework in Phase II and IV. The distinction between monitoring (upward) and control (downward) information flow provided the precise architecture for the decoupling analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational reciprocal teaching study. Produced some of the largest effect sizes in comprehension instruction research. Analyzed here as evidence for the externalization principle: the social externalization of monitoring was the key architectural change.

> [!cite] Reference
> Strong evidence for the self-explanation effect in instructional contexts, extending the Chi et al. findings. Supports the externalization principle and the claim that it is the monitoring-externalization component of self-explanation (not elaboration per se) that drives comprehension gains.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of the situation model literature, establishing the five dimensions of situational coherence monitoring (temporal, causal, spatial, protagonist, intentional). Supports the three-tier monitoring model's Tier 3 specification.

### From: [[reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20|reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper proposing that skilled reading requires automaticity in lower-level processes (letter and word recognition) so that attention can be devoted to comprehension. Introduced the concept of automatic processing as a prerequisite for fluent reading and shaped subsequent fluency research for five decades. Referenced in Phases II and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original presentation of the Simple View of Reading. Brief but theoretically important for establishing the two-component architecture of reading comprehension and the diagnostic clarity it provides for reading disability classification. Referenced in Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal paper establishing the Matthew Effect in reading development. Synthesized longitudinal and cross-sectional evidence for the compounding inequality of early reading skill and its consequences for vocabulary, world knowledge, and cognitive development. One of the most cited papers in educational psychology. Referenced in Phases III and VII.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph developing verbal efficiency theory. Establishes the working memory mechanism of the fluency-comprehension link and provides the most precise account of how local text processing efficiency determines comprehension quality. Referenced in Phases II and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Influential paper providing the three-component (accuracy, rate, prosody) definition of reading fluency and a comprehensive review of fluency intervention approaches. Also presents Wolf's RAVE-O program (Retrieval, Automaticity, Vocabulary elaboration, Engagement with language, Orthography) as a multidimensional fluency intervention. Referenced in Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive government-commissioned meta-analysis of reading research that identified phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension as the five pillars of reading instruction. Established the evidentiary basis for guided oral reading as an effective fluency intervention. Referenced in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original paper describing the repeated reading technique for fluency development. Demonstrated that multiple readings of short passages to a fluency criterion produce both within-text fluency gains and generalization to new text — providing evidence for automatization of processing capabilities rather than text-specific memorization. Referenced in Phases III and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph developing the multi-component model of working memory including the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive. Provides the theoretical basis for understanding how decoding resource demands interact with sentence-level integration. Referenced in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive meta-analytic review of fluency development and intervention research. Established effect sizes for guided oral reading on fluency and comprehension outcomes and identified the conditions under which fluency interventions are most effective. Referenced in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Practical and theoretical synthesis of fluency research with instructional applications. Emphasizes prosody as a component of fluency and its relationship to comprehension. Accessible treatment for practitioners building on the same research base. Referenced in Phases II and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational model of text comprehension as the construction of a situation model. Critical for understanding why fluency impairment affects not just word-level processing but the coherence of the mental representation of text content. Referenced in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Philosophical work developing the focal-subsidiary attention distinction — the concept that skilled performance involves attending through tools (words, instruments, syntax) to goals (meaning, music, argument), with the tools operating subsidiarily rather than focally. Provides the phenomenological framework for the automaticity transition in reading. Referenced in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational work distinguishing knowledge-telling from knowledge-transforming writing strategies. Provides the empirical basis for the bottleneck analysis in Phase VII: writing mechanics non-automaticity drives knowledge-telling as a low-cognitive-demand default strategy. Referenced in Phase VII.

### From: [[schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22|schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical work demonstrating schema-guided reconstruction in memory. Bartlett's serial reproduction paradigm remains the clearest experimental evidence for schemas operating — and is, as this report argues, silent on how those schemas were formed. Essential for Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The "schema timing" paradigm demonstrating that schemas must be active *during* comprehension to be effective. The post-context condition evidence directly motivates the Schema-Timing Paradox in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The chess chunk studies providing the clearest evidence for schema construction through expertise development. The 50,000 chunks estimate and the 10-year rule are central to Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper on Structural Mapping Theory, providing the theoretical framework for the analogy-as-scaffolding mechanism analyzed in Phase IV. The core of the Analogy-First Instructional Principle.

> [!cite] Reference
> Piaget's developmental account of schema construction through sensorimotor interaction. The stage transition evidence is central to Phase III's claim that schema construction has a discontinuous character. The equilibration mechanism is the developmental precursor to the crystallization event model.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical Schema Theory formalization. The three-mode learning model (accretion, tuning, restructuring) is the theoretical foundation for this report's analysis of the construction-activation asymmetry.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original three-mode learning taxonomy. Restructuring receives the thinnest theoretical development of the three modes — a fact central to this report's diagnosis of the construction-activation asymmetry.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational advance organizer paper. The analysis here argues that advance organizers address degree novelty but are insufficient for categorical novelty, which requires structural analogue pre-teaching.

> [!cite] Reference
> The deliberate practice framework extending the chess chunking findings to expertise development broadly. Provides the longitudinal time-scale evidence for schema construction.

### From: [[silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21|silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive scientific review of speed-reading claims against the eye movement evidence base. Systematically examines RSVP, peripheral vision claims, and sub-vocalization suppression. Supports Phases II and III. Essential reading for anyone evaluating commercial speed-reading products.

> [!cite] Reference
> Presents the Lexical Quality Hypothesis — the theoretical foundation for the vocabulary automaticity argument in Phase IV. Argues that the quality of lexical representations (their precision, completeness, and consolidation) is the primary determinant of reading ability differences. Strongly supports the highest-leverage intervention recommendation.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original articulation of the distinction between propositional text base and situation model, foundational for Phase II's definition of comprehension. Provides the theoretical grounding for why surface-level reading metrics are insufficient as comprehension standards.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of reading rate research establishing the empirical base rate of skilled adult reading speed. Introduces the concept of "rauding" (efficient reading at the optimal rate for comprehension), providing the theoretical foundation for the comprehension-indexed speed metric. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive summary of two decades of eye movement research in reading, establishing the perceptual span constraint and fixation duration norms. Foundational for Phase III's evidence review on the mechanics of skilled reading.

> [!cite] Reference
> Updated working memory model including the episodic buffer, directly relevant to the situation model construction bottleneck. Provides theoretical grounding for Phase IV's treatment of working memory integration demands during reading.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original advance organizer study, providing empirical grounding for the prior knowledge pre-activation strategy in Phase V. Demonstrably improves both comprehension and retention through schema pre-activation.

> [!cite] Reference
> Theoretical account of how experts use long-term memory structures to extend effective working memory capacity in their domain. Provides the mechanistic bridge between domain expertise and the reading speed advantage — supports the schema-as-inference-engine analysis in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational for the [[fluency-illusion]] analysis — demonstrates that subjective ease of processing is negatively correlated with actual learning depth. Directly relevant to the speed-monitoring incompatibility and the risks of bandwidth compression training.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive framework for the types of inferences generated during reading and their resource demands. Provides the inferential bridging analysis underpinning Bottleneck 3 in Phase II, and grounds the prior knowledge pre-activation argument of Phase V.

---

## Cognitive Psychology/Metacognition

### From: [[metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20|metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational treatment of desirable difficulties as interventions that improve long-term learning by replacing fluency cues with retrieval cues. Directly supports the cue invalidity mechanism (Phase IV). Foundational for [[desirable-difficulties]] node.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original framework paper introducing the metacognitive knowledge/experience/monitoring architecture. The source of the [[flavell-metacognition-framework]] node. Supports the three-component framework in Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> Established the cue-utilization framework for metacognitive monitoring, demonstrating that JOLs are based on accessible cues rather than direct introspection. Core empirical support for the cue invalidity mechanism (Phase IV).

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational Dunning-Kruger paper, documenting expertise-dependent monitoring accuracy. Supports the argument that monitoring accuracy is domain-specific and develops alongside domain knowledge (Phase III).

> [!cite] Reference
> The meta-level/object-level model of metacognition. Defines the monitoring/control distinction foundational to the coupling analysis in Phase II. See [[Meta-Level-Object-Level-Model]].

> [!cite] Reference
> Empirical demonstration of the fluency-performance dissociation: disfluency lowers comprehension judgments while improving actual performance. Core evidence for the fluency illusion mechanism (Phase III).

> [!cite] Reference
> Documented the monitoring gap: students who accurately detected poor comprehension nonetheless defaulted to re-reading. The key empirical anchor for coupling failure distinct from monitoring failure (Phase III).

> [!cite] Reference
> COPES model of self-regulated learning, identifying the cognitive processing steps between monitoring signals and regulatory action. Direct theoretical support for the threshold mechanism in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Zimmerman's three-phase SRL model. The analysis in Phase VI maps this model onto the infrastructure framework, deepening its theoretical account. See [[Zimmerman's-Three-Phase-SRL-Cycle]].

> [!cite] Reference
> Extends Flavell's framework to regulation processes, distinguishing monitoring from control functions. Core theoretical support for the three-component architecture in Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive synthesis of empirical research on metacognition in educational contexts. The most authoritative reference for the monitoring-regulation relationship and its instructional implications. Supports Phases III and V broadly.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational treatment of near and far transfer, including the conditions under which structural insights from one domain travel to others. The theoretical grounding for the FAR transfer architecture in Phase VII.

---

## Cognitive Science

### From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph. Establishes the theoretical framework, the contrast with rule-based theories, the empirical program, and the connection to model-theoretic semantics. Essential reading — but note that subsequent work has substantially revised the theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> The major empirical program. Systematic investigation of syllogistic, propositional, and relational reasoning from the mental models perspective. Contains the most complete presentation of the model-number predictions.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary theoretical alternative. Develops the mental logic account (PSYCOP) in detail. Essential for understanding what mental models theory is *contrasted against*; also contains important methodological critiques.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of the empirical literature. Remains a standard reference for the difficulty effects, belief-bias findings, and comparative performance of models and rules.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces pragmatic reasoning schemas — the main alternative that accommodates content effects within a rule-based framework. Directly challenges the interpretation of facilitation effects as evidence for mental models.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes the probabilistic rational analysis framework for the Wason selection task. Argues that human performance is near-optimal under realistic probabilistic assumptions — the most important challenge to both mental models and mental logic accounts.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical argument that apparent analog properties of mental imagery could be artifacts of tacit knowledge rather than evidence of a genuinely analog representational format. Applies directly to the analogical status of mental models.

> [!cite] Reference
> Develops the situation model framework in discourse comprehension — the closest empirical tradition to mental models theory with stronger methodological development and broader empirical coverage.

> [!cite] Reference

## Cognitive Science Of Reading

### From: [[reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28|reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical study establishing the 5–15% incidental acquisition rate from natural reading. This study's methodology — having students read naturalistic passages and testing acquisition of words appearing in those passages — defined the paradigm for two decades of subsequent research. Its finding that directive context quality mediates acquisition rates is this report's primary evidential foundation.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The method section (for understanding how acquisition rate is operationalized) and the discussion section's analysis of context quality factors.
> 
> **Historical Significance:** This paper initiated the quantitative paradigm for vocabulary acquisition research and provided the first systematic rate estimates that made Matthew Effect projections possible.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary source for the 98% coverage threshold concept. Nation and Waring's analysis of English frequency distributions and their implications for text coverage provides the empirical grounding for the threshold concept used throughout this report. Their frequency band analysis (first 1,000, 2,000, etc. word families) is still the standard reference for coverage estimation.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The section on coverage and text density, and the practical implications for extensive reading programs.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that coined the term "Matthew Effect" in reading and provided the initial empirical documentation of compounding advantage in reading achievement. Stanovich synthesized a disparate empirical literature into a coherent developmental model. The paper is both an empirical review and a theoretical proposal, and its central claim — that early vocabulary and reading skill differences compound rather than narrow over time — has been extensively replicated.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The sections on print exposure, vocabulary development, and the full model of reading development.

> [!cite] Reference
> Perfetti's most complete statement of the Lexical Quality Hypothesis — the theory that underlies the Cognitive Slack Model's working memory claims. This paper articulates how lexical quality (the precision and completeness of word representations in memory) affects reading comprehension through its effects on working memory demands during word recognition.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The sections on lexical quality dimensions and the relationship between lexical quality and comprehension.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original context quality taxonomy study, providing empirical evidence that naturally occurring contexts vary dramatically in their inferential supportiveness. This study's finding — that misdirective and non-directive contexts together account for approximately half of naturally occurring contexts for unfamiliar words — is one of the most practically consequential findings for understanding the limits of reading-based vocabulary acquisition.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The context taxonomy coding scheme and the distribution of context types across the sample texts.

> [!cite] Reference
> The study that systematically varied the number of exposures to target words and found that acquisition plateaued at approximately 10 exposures — but only under conditions of high context quality. This finding grounds the analysis of the frequency-quality interaction and provides empirical support for the depth-frequency multiplier.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The multi-exposure conditions table and the discussion of context quality as a moderator.

> [!cite] Reference
> A meta-analysis of 20 studies on incidental vocabulary acquisition from reading, confirming the 15% per-encounter mean rate for contextually inferable words and documenting the moderating role of prior vocabulary knowledge. Particularly valuable for its cross-study analysis of moderating variables.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The forest plot of acquisition rates across studies and the moderator analysis section.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper for the depth-of-processing framework used in Phase IV. While not specifically a vocabulary study, its core claim — that elaborative semantic processing produces more durable memory representations than surface processing — is the theoretical foundation for the depth-frequency multiplier.
> 
> **Historical Significance:** This paper was one of the most influential in cognitive psychology, initiating a research program on encoding conditions that transformed memory research and educational application.

> [!cite] Reference
> Nagy and Anderson's frequency analysis of English school texts, establishing estimates of total word family counts and the distribution of morphologically related word families. This study grounds the morphological leverage analysis by providing empirical evidence that a small number of root morphemes underlies a large proportion of the academic vocabulary.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The morphological family analysis section and the discussion of implications for instruction.

> [!cite] Reference
> The print exposure study that provided the first systematic evidence of the Matthew Effect's scale — documenting the enormous differences in annual print exposure between high and low achievement readers and their vocabulary growth implications. The arithmetic in this report's Phase V draws directly on their exposure estimates.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The exposure estimates table and the vocabulary growth projection section.

---

## Cognitive Science/Learning Theory

### From: [[cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22|cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper introducing the three-component model of cognitive load (intrinsic, extraneous, germane). Supports Phase II's analytical framework and the definition of germane load. Essential background for understanding the theoretical architecture this analysis critiques.

> [!cite] Reference
> The key reconceptualization paper challenging the three-component model and proposing a two-component alternative grounded in element interactivity. Central to Phase III's evidence analysis and Phase IV's mechanistic account.

> [!cite] Reference
> Reformulates CLT's intrinsic load concept in terms of element interactivity and provides the mechanistic architecture that enables the Kalyuga reconceptualization. Central to Phase IV's mechanistic analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> Documents the expertise reversal effect and its instructional implications. Primary evidence for the conclusion that load type is indexed to knowledge state. Phase IV's mechanistic analysis draws heavily on this.

> [!cite] Reference
> The variability of practice study most directly motivating the germane load construct. Important for Phase III's evidence section as the strongest empirical case for productive cognitive effort. The kinematics version (1994b) is also relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The validated measurement instrument for the three load types. Phase III's measurement crisis analysis draws on this work to show that independent measurement of germane load remains elusive even with validated instruments.

> [!cite] Reference
> A careful critique of CLT's theoretical and empirical limitations, including the measurement challenges. Supports Phase III's critical analysis and Phase V's limitations discussion.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive book-length treatment of CLT from its primary architects. Incorporates the element interactivity reformulation and partially acknowledges the germane load critique while defending a version of the three-component model.

> [!cite] Reference
> Reviews CLT's applications to complex learning tasks and the 4C/ID model. Relevant to Phase V's practical implications for adaptive instructional design.

> [!cite] Reference
> Overview of CLT's development at the point of its broadest practical uptake. Useful for understanding the theory's scope and ambitions at the time the germane load construct became standard.

> [!cite] Reference
> Revises Miller's 7±2 to approximately 4 independent chunks, providing the more precise working memory capacity constraint that CLT's claims rest on. Referenced in Phase IV's schema formation cascade.

---

## Communication Psychology

### From: [[how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03|how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03]]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Aristotle. *Rhetoric*. Translated by George A. Kennedy. Oxford University Press, 1991. [Foundational text; logos/ethos/pathos framework]
> - Shannon, C. E., & Weaver, W. (1949). *The Mathematical Theory of Communication*. University of Illinois Press. [Foundational transmission model; [[Shannon-Weaver-Information-Theory]]]
> - Watzlawick, P., Beavin, J. H., & Jackson, D. D. (1967). *Pragmatics of Human Communication*. W. W. Norton. [Axioms of communication; relationship/content distinction]
> - Ausubel, D. P. (1960). The use of advance organizers in the learning and retention of meaningful verbal material. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 51(5), 267–272. [Advance organizers; [[advance-organizers]]]
> - Sweller, J. (1988). Cognitive load during problem solving: Effects on learning. *Cognitive Science*, 12(2), 257–285. [Foundational CLT; [[cognitive-load-theory]]]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Salovey, P., & Mayer, J. D. (1990). Emotional intelligence. *Imagination, Cognition and Personality*, 9(3), 185–211. [Original EI framework; [[emotional-intelligence]]]
> - Goleman, D. (1995). *Emotional Intelligence*. Bantam Books. [EI popularization and communication applications]
> - Barrett, L. F. (2017). *How Emotions Are Made: The Secret Life of the Brain*. Houghton Mifflin Harcourt. [Emotional granularity; [[emotional-granularity]]]
> - Galinsky, A. D., & Ku, G. (2004). The effects of perspective-taking on prejudice: The moderating role of self-evaluation. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*, 30(5), 594–604. [Perspective-taking research]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Hattie, J. (2009). *Visible Learning: A Synthesis of Over 800 Meta-Analyses Relating to Achievement*. Routledge. [Meta-synthesis of feedback research; [[Hattie-&-Timperley-Feedback-Model]]]
> - Hattie, J., & Timperley, H. (2007). The power of feedback. *Review of Educational Research*, 77(1), 81–112. [Foundational four-level feedback model; [[feedback-design]]]
> - Edmondson, A. C. (1999). Psychological safety and learning behavior in work teams. *Administrative Science Quarterly*, 44(2), 350–383. [Psychological safety construct; [[psychological-safety]]]
> - Argyris, C., & Schön, D. A. (1978). *Organizational Learning: A Theory of Action Perspective*. Addison-Wesley. [Double-loop learning; [[Argyris-Double-Loop-Learning]]]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Toulmin, S. E. (1958). *The Uses of Argument*. Cambridge University Press. [Toulmin argument model; [[argumentation-theory]]]
> - Mercier, H., & Sperber, D. (2017). *The Enigma of Reason*. Harvard University Press. [Epistemic vigilance; [[epistemic-vigilance]]; argumentative theory of reasoning]
> - van der Linden, S., & Roozenbeek, J. (2020). Psychological inoculation against fake news. In R. Greifeneder et al. (Eds.), *The Social Psychology of Misinformation*. Routledge. [Inoculation theory; [[inoculation-theory]]]

## Critical Thinking

### From: [[critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01|critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01]]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Dewey, J. (1910). *How We Think*. D.C. Heath and Company. — The founding text of modern CT theory; introduced reflective thinking as the educational goal.
> - Ennis, R.H. (1962). A concept of critical thinking. *Harvard Educational Review*, 32(1), 81-111. — First modern analytical definition of CT; launched the contemporary research programme.
> - Paul, R. (1990). *Critical Thinking: What Every Person Needs to Survive in a Rapidly Changing World*. Foundation for Critical Thinking. — Introduced the strong-sense/weak-sense distinction and the structural framework of Elements, Standards, Traits.
> - Facione, P.A. (1990). *Critical Thinking: A Statement of Expert Consensus for Purposes of Educational Assessment and Instruction (The Delphi Report)*. California Academic Press. — Consensus definition and PENCRISAL taxonomy from 46 experts.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Kahneman, D. (2011). *Thinking, Fast and Slow*. Farrar, Straus and Giroux. — Definitive popular treatment of dual-process theory and heuristics/biases research.
> - Stanovich, K.E. (2011). *Rationality and the Reflective Mind*. Oxford University Press. — Distinguishes algorithmic and reflective minds within System 2; central to understanding individual differences in CT.
> - Tversky, A., & Kahneman, D. (1974). Judgment under uncertainty: Heuristics and biases. *Science*, 185(4157), 1124-1131. — Landmark paper establishing the heuristics and biases research programme.
> - Nickerson, R.S. (1998). Confirmation bias: A ubiquitous phenomenon in many guises. *Review of General Psychology*, 2(2), 175-220. — Comprehensive review of confirmation bias research.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Flavell, J.H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906-911. — Foundational paper defining metacognition and its components.
> - Nelson, T.O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125-173. — Monitoring-control model of metacognition.
> - Kuhn, D. (1999). A developmental model of critical thinking. *Educational Researcher*, 28(2), 16-46. — CT as metacognitive development; realist-absolutist-multiplist-evaluativist progression.
> - Zimmerman, B.J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts et al. (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation*. Academic Press. — Foundational treatment of SRL.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Perkins, D.N., & Salomon, G. (1989). Are cognitive skills context-bound? *Educational Researcher*, 18(1), 16-25. — Near/far transfer distinction; mindful abstraction as key to transfer.
> - Barnett, S.M., & Ceci, S.J. (2002). When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer. *Psychological Bulletin*, 128(4), 612-637. — Six-dimension transfer taxonomy.
> - Abrami, P.C., et al. (2008). Instructional interventions affecting critical thinking skills and dispositions: A Stage 1 meta-analysis. *Review of Educational Research*, 78(4), 1102-1134. — Meta-analysis supporting mixed approach pedagogy.
> - Halpern, D.F. (1998). Teaching critical thinking for transfer across domains. *American Psychologist*, 53(4), 449-455. — Framework for explicit transfer instruction.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Baehr, J. (2011). *The Inquiring Mind: On Intellectual Virtues and Virtue Epistemology*. Oxford University Press. — Defines intellectual virtues and their role in epistemic agency.
> - Battaly, H. (2015). *Virtue*. Polity Press. — Distinguishes reliabilist and responsibilist virtue epistemology.
> - McPeck, J.E. (1981). *Critical Thinking and Education*. Blackwell. — The strongest articulation of the domain-specificity position.

### From: [[paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01|paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational statement of weak-sense vs. strong-sense distinction; central to trait-dependent account of critical thinking.

> [!cite] Reference
> Canonical concise presentation of Elements, Standards, and Traits used in instructional implementation.

> [!cite] Reference
> Extended pedagogical translation of framework into classroom and self-directed learning contexts.

> [!cite] Reference
> Classical account of reflective thought as active, persistent inquiry; major precursor to modern critical thinking pedagogy.

> [!cite] Reference
> Influential analytical framework distinguishing dispositions and abilities in critical thinking.

> [!cite] Reference
> Widely cited consensus definition and skills-dispositions model in assessment contexts.

> [!cite] Reference
> Transfer-focused critical thinking pedagogy emphasizing metacognitive and contextualized practice.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational theory on near/far transfer and conditions supporting transferability.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive transfer taxonomy helpful for evaluating critical thinking beyond classroom settings.

> [!cite] Reference
> Developmental and dialogic perspective on reasoning competence and argument.

> [!cite] Reference
> Critiques skill atomism and advances a richer account of critical thinking as norm-governed practice.

> [!cite] Reference
> Philosophical grounding for trait-centered accounts of intellectual character relevant to Paul-Elder's dispositional model.

> [!cite] Reference
> Not a Paul-Elder text, but critical for understanding bias structures that motivate explicit standards-based checks.

> [!cite] Reference
> Empirical grounding for distinctions between intelligence, reflective disposition, and rational thought quality.

## Educational Philosophy

### From: [[pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01|pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01]]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Dewey, J. (1910/1933). *How We Think: A Restatement of the Relation of Reflective Thinking to the Educative Process*. Boston: D.C. Heath. — The definitive statement of Dewey's reflective thinking model.
> - Dewey, J. (1916). *Democracy and Education: An Introduction to the Philosophy of Education*. New York: Macmillan. — Dewey's most comprehensive work on education, growth, and democracy.
> - Dewey, J. (1938). *Experience and Education*. New York: Kappa Delta Pi. — The distilled statement of Dewey's educational philosophy, organized around continuity and interaction.
> - Peirce, C.S. (1878). "How to Make Our Ideas Clear." *Popular Science Monthly*, 12, 286–302. — The originating text of pragmatism and the pragmatic maxim.
> - James, W. (1907). *Pragmatism: A New Name for Some Old Ways of Thinking*. New York: Longmans, Green. — James's popular lectures defining his version of pragmatism and pragmatist truth-theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Bernstein, R.J. (2010). *The Pragmatic Turn*. Cambridge: Polity Press. — Comprehensive treatment of pragmatism's contemporary revival and its relationship to Continental philosophy.
> - Menand, L. (2001). *The Metaphysical Club: A Story of Ideas in America*. New York: Farrar, Straus and Giroux. — Pulitzer Prize-winning intellectual history of the origins of pragmatism.
> - Rorty, R. (1979). *Philosophy and the Mirror of Nature*. Princeton: Princeton University Press. — The landmark neo-pragmatist critique of representationalism.
> - Hickman, L.A. (1990). *John Dewey's Pragmatic Technology*. Bloomington: Indiana University Press. — Analysis of Dewey's instrumentalism and its implications for technology and inquiry.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Hmelo-Silver, C.E., Duncan, R.G., & Chinn, C.A. (2007). "Scaffolding and Achievement in Problem-Based and Inquiry Learning." *Educational Psychologist*, 42(2), 99–107. — Meta-analytic evidence on the effectiveness of scaffolded inquiry.
> - Schon, D.A. (1983). *The Reflective Practitioner: How Professionals Think in Action*. New York: Basic Books. — The foundational text on reflection-in-action in professional contexts.
> - Argyris, C. & Schon, D.A. (1978). *Organizational Learning: A Theory of Action Perspective*. Reading, MA: Addison-Wesley. — The originating text on single- and double-loop learning.
> - Halpern, D.F. (1998). "Teaching Critical Thinking for Transfer Across Domains." *American Psychologist*, 53(4), 449–455. — Empirical framework for teaching transferable critical thinking skills.
> - Perkins, D.N. & Salomon, G. (1989). "Are Cognitive Skills Context-Bound?" *Educational Researcher*, 18(1), 16–25. — Influential taxonomy of low-road and high-road transfer mechanisms.
> - Flavell, J.H. (1979). "Metacognition and Cognitive Monitoring: A New Area of Cognitive-Developmental Inquiry." *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911. — The article that launched metacognition as a research program.
> - Pintrich, P.R. (2000). "The Role of Goal Orientation in Self-Regulated Learning." In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). San Diego: Academic Press. — Integrative framework connecting motivation and self-regulation.
> - Kapur, M. (2008). "Productive Failure." *Cognition and Instruction*, 26(3), 379–424. — Empirical evidence that initial failure on complex problems enhances deeper learning.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Dewey, J. (1910/1933). *How We Think: A Restatement of the Relation of Reflective Thinking to the Educative Process*. Boston: D.C. Heath. — The definitive statement of Dewey's reflective thinking model.

> - Schon, D.A. (1983). *The Reflective Practitioner: How Professionals Think in Action*. New York: Basic Books. — The foundational text on reflection-in-action in professional contexts.
> - Argyris, C. & Schon, D.A. (1978). *Organizational Learning: A Theory of Action Perspective*. Reading, MA: Addison-Wesley. — The originating text on single- and double-loop learning.
> - Halpern, D.F. (1998). "Teaching Critical Thinking for Transfer Across Domains." *American Psychologist*, 53(4), 449–455. — Empirical framework for teaching transferable critical thinking skills.
> - Perkins, D.N. & Salomon, G. (1989). "Are Cognitive Skills Context-Bound?" *Educational Researcher*, 18(1), 16–25. — Influential taxonomy of low-road and high-road transfer mechanisms.
> - Flavell, J.H. (1979). "Metacognition and Cognitive Monitoring: A New Area of Cognitive-Developmental Inquiry." *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911. — The article that launched metacognition as a research program.
> - Pintrich, P.R. (2000). "The Role of Goal Orientation in Self-Regulated Learning." In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). San Diego: Academic Press. — Integrative framework connecting motivation and self-regulation.
> - Kapur, M. (2008). "Productive Failure." *Cognition and Instruction*, 26(3), 379–424. — Empirical evidence that initial failure on complex problems enhances deeper learning.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Dewey, J. (1910/1933). *How We Think: A Restatement of the Relation of Reflective Thinking to the Educative Process*. Boston: D.C. Heath. — The definitive statement of Dewey's reflective thinking model.
> - Dewey, J. (1916). *Democracy and Education: An Introduction to the Philosophy of Education*. New York: Macmillan. — Dewey's most comprehensive work on education, growth, and democracy.
> - Dewey, J. (1938). *Experience and Education*. New York: Kappa Delta Pi. — The distilled statement of Dewey's educational philosophy, organized around continuity and interaction.
> - Peirce, C.S. (1878). "How to Make Our Ideas Clear." *Popular Science Monthly*, 12, 286–302. — The originating text of pragmatism and the pragmatic maxim.
> - James, W. (1907). *Pragmatism: A New Name for Some Old Ways of Thinking*. New York: Longmans, Green. — James's popular lectures defining his version of pragmatism and pragmatist truth-theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Bernstein, R.J. (2010). *The Pragmatic Turn*. Cambridge: Polity Press. — Comprehensive treatment of pragmatism's contemporary revival and its relationship to Continental philosophy.
> - Menand, L. (2001). *The Metaphysical Club: A Story of Ideas in America*. New York: Farrar, Straus and Giroux. — Pulitzer Prize-winning intellectual history of the origins of pragmatism.
> - Rorty, R. (1979). *Philosophy and the Mirror of Nature*. Princeton: Princeton University Press. — The landmark neo-pragmatist critique of representationalism.
> - Hickman, L.A. (1990). *John Dewey's Pragmatic Technology*. Bloomington: Indiana University Press. — Analysis of Dewey's instrumentalism and its implications for technology and inquiry.

> [!cite] Reference
> - Hmelo-Silver, C.E., Duncan, R.G., & Chinn, C.A. (2007). "Scaffolding and Achievement in Problem-Based and Inquiry Learning." *Educational Psychologist*, 42(2), 99–107. — Meta-analytic evidence on the effectiveness of scaffolded inquiry.
> - Schon, D.A. (1983). *The Reflective Practitioner: How Professionals Think in Action*. New York: Basic Books. — The foundational text on reflection-in-action in professional contexts.
> - Argyris, C. & Schon, D.A. (1978). *Organizational Learning: A Theory of Action Perspective*. Reading, MA: Addison-Wesley. — The originating text on single- and double-loop learning.
> - Halpern, D.F. (1998). "Teaching Critical Thinking for Transfer Across Domains." *American Psychologist*, 53(4), 449–455. — Empirical framework for teaching transferable critical thinking skills.
> - Perkins, D.N. & Salomon, G. (1989). "Are Cognitive Skills Context-Bound?" *Educational Researcher*, 18(1), 16–25. — Influential taxonomy of low-road and high-road transfer mechanisms.
> - Flavell, J.H. (1979). "Metacognition and Cognitive Monitoring: A New Area of Cognitive-Developmental Inquiry." *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911. — The article that launched metacognition as a research program.
> - Pintrich, P.R. (2000). "The Role of Goal Orientation in Self-Regulated Learning." In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). San Diego: Academic Press. — Integrative framework connecting motivation and self-regulation.

## Educational Psychology

### From: [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10|motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive theoretical statement of SDT across domains. Directly relevant to this report's entire motivational architecture analysis (Section 4 and Tier 3 strategies). Chapters on academic motivation and internalization provide the primary empirical and theoretical basis for the regulatory quality framework and basic needs analysis in reading contexts. Recommended reading: Chapters 6 (academic motivation), 8 (internalization), and 14 (self-regulation).

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive theoretical statement of the construction-integration model and the three-level text representation framework. Foundational to this report's cognitive architecture analysis (Section 2). Provides the theoretical basis for the distinction between textbase and situation model processing that underlies the entire comprehension-oriented strategy analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive reference on self-regulated learning across domains. Relevant to this report's Section 7 and metacognitive self-regulation analysis. Provides extensive empirical grounding for the cyclical SRL model applied to reading and academic work. Key chapters: Zimmerman on self-regulation of learning, Pintrich on goal orientation and self-regulation.

> [!cite] Reference
> Seminal empirical study establishing the Motivations for Reading Questionnaire (MRQ) and documenting the multidimensional structure of reading motivation. Foundation for the Section 3 treatment of reading motivation's multidimensional nature. Demonstrated that different motivational dimensions (challenge, curiosity, involvement, importance, recognition, grades, competition, social, compliance, efficacy) predict reading behavior independently.

> [!cite] Reference
> Primary source for the four-phase interest development model. Directly foundational to the Section 3 treatment of interest theory and the Tier 3 interest-building reading program strategy. Provides the theoretical basis for distinguishing situational from individual interest and specifying the developmental transitions between phases.

> [!cite] Reference
> Primary source for the desirable difficulties framework. Foundational to the Tier 2 strategy analysis, particularly spaced repetition, retrieval practice, and the cognitive load calibration analysis. Provides the theoretical basis for recommending effortful learning conditions despite their short-term costs.

> [!cite] Reference
> Seminal empirical demonstration of self-explanation effects on comprehension and learning. Foundational to the Tier 2 self-explanation strategy recommendation. Demonstrated that students who self-explained during learning achieved significantly higher comprehension than those who re-read the same material.

> [!cite] Reference
> Primary source for the 2×2 achievement goal framework. Foundational to this report's Section 5 analysis. Demonstrates the distinct motivational and performance patterns associated with mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals.

> [!cite] Reference
> Primary source and original empirical validation of the reciprocal teaching method. Foundational to this report's Section 7 treatment of social metacognition and collaborative reading. Demonstrated significant comprehension improvements from reciprocal teaching in comparison conditions.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational empirical demonstration of the testing effect (retrieval practice advantage over re-reading). Directly foundational to the Tier 2 retrieval practice strategy recommendation and the calibration training analysis in Section 7.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive theoretical statement of SDT across domains. Directly relevant to this report's entire motivational architecture analysis (Section 4 and Tier 3 strategies). ## Educational Psychology & Learning Science

### From: [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03|generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]]

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: The foundational paper in which Wittrock first articulated the generative learning model. Proposes that learning involves the active construction of relationships between new information and prior knowledge; introduces the core SOI framework in nascent form. *Essential primary source.*

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: The canonical theoretical elaboration of GLT, applying the model to reading comprehension and identifying the specific generative strategies (summaries, analogies, questions, drawings) that engage SOI processes. Required reading for any serious engagement with the theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: The seminal empirical demonstration of the generation effect. Established the paradigmatic experimental design used in hundreds of subsequent studies and provided the foundational empirical anchor for GLT's core claim about active versus passive encoding.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: The landmark study documenting the self-explanation effect through think-aloud protocols with undergraduate physics students. First systematic demonstration that student-generated explanations of worked examples produce superior transfer, independent of problem-solving time invested.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: The comprehensive theoretical and empirical account of CTML, synthesizing over 100 experimental studies deriving and testing the twelve multimedia learning principles. Provides the most extensive evidence base for GLT-derived instructional design prescriptions in multimedia environments.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: Presents the ICAP framework and its supporting evidence, providing a behavioral taxonomy aligned with GLT's generativity hierarchy. Distinguishes Interactive, Constructive, Active, and Passive modes of cognitive engagement and proposes differential learning outcomes.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: A key empirical paper demonstrating that pre-instruction problem exploration (which typically produces failure) before direct instruction produces superior learning compared to conventional instruction-first sequences. Extends GTL principles to instructional sequencing.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: The seminal modern demonstration of the testing effect. Shows that repeated retrieval practice substantially outperforms repeated study for improving long-term retention, connecting retrieval practice to GLT's generative encoding principles.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: Foundational chapter articulating the desirable difficulties concept, explaining why learning conditions that feel more effortful often produce superior long-term retention and transfer. Essential conceptual bridge between GLT and cognitive load theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: Demonstrates that longhand note-taking produces superior conceptual learning compared to verbatim laptop transcription, providing ecological evidence for GLT's note-making prescriptions in realistic classroom contexts.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: Meta-analytic review of the self-explanation literature, finding robust effects (d ≈ 0.55) with significant moderation by content type and the nature of self-explanation prompting. Essential calibration of the self-explanation effect across the wider literature.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Annotation**: Systematic review of the transfer literature developing a taxonomy of near-to-far transfer dimensions. Provides the theoretical framework for analyzing the transferability of GLT principles across domains and the conditions that maximize transfer distance.

> [!cite] Reference

## Educational Psychology — Motivation And Self Regulation

### From: [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15|goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational work on the social psychology of creativity, providing the empirical basis for the claim that controlling motivational environments undermine creative output — relevant to the far transfer discussion of creative practice.

> [!cite] Reference
> Systematic taxonomy of transfer distance and context, providing the theoretical grounding for the Far Transfer section's analysis of structural principle abstraction.

> [!cite] Reference
> The landmark review establishing the evidence base for formative assessment's impact on learning, foundational to Section 5's assessment architecture.

> [!cite] Reference
> The authoritative theoretical statement of SDT's integrative framework, providing the motivational architecture for Section 2's analysis and the autonomy-preservation principles of Section 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal articulation of the mastery vs. performance goal distinction and its implications for learning persistence and strategy use — the conceptual foundation for the mastery orientation principle throughout this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> Research on the conditions under which far transfer is achievable, emphasising the importance of abstracting structural principles — employed as the theoretical basis for the Far Transfer section.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive meta-analytic review of feedback effectiveness, establishing the four-level model (task, process, self-regulation, self) and the evidence that process and self-regulation feedback are most effective — directly applied in Section 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> The authors' own comprehensive review of Goal-Setting Theory's development, evidence base, and boundary conditions — the primary source for Section 1's treatment.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational statement of the two-level metacognitive monitoring framework employed throughout Section 4 and as a vertex of the GRM Triad.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive overview of Pintrich's 4×4 SRL framework complementing Zimmerman's cyclical model — employed in Section 3's analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The authoritative monograph presenting SDT's mature theoretical architecture, providing the comprehensive reference for the motivational framework employed throughout this report.

> [!cite] Reference
