## Educational Psychology

### From: [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24|formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational synthesis establishing the empirical case for formative assessment's large effect sizes. Phases II and III rely on this work for evidence about variance across studies and the premium of active-engagement practices. Essential starting point for any serious study of formative assessment.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most carefully theorized framework for understanding why different types of feedback produce different outcomes. The four-level taxonomy and the self-regulation-level premium are central to Phase III's evidence review. Essential reading for anyone designing feedback systems.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational theoretical paper establishing the monitoring-control architecture that the MCM builds on. Phase IV's mechanism analysis is structurally organized around this framework. Required reading for understanding the cognitive architecture of calibration.

> [!cite] Reference
> Direct evidence that retrieval practice produces calibration gains independent of retention gains — a key piece of evidence for the MCM's claim that the testing effect's mechanism includes a calibration component. Phase III draws on this finding.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive treatment of the metacognitive calibration literature. Provides the empirical foundation for the MCM's claims about monitoring accuracy and its role in learning. Essential reference for Phase IV's mechanism analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> Direct evidence that metacognitive accuracy can be improved through specific activities (delayed summarization) — evidence that monitoring accuracy is not fixed and that calibration events can be designed. Phase IV draws on this work.

> [!cite] Reference
> The pre-Black & Wiliam foundational paper that first characterized formative assessment in terms of the gap between current and desired performance. Provides the intellectual ancestry for both the ITM and MCM, and is essential for understanding the historical development of the field.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational study on comprehension monitoring training, demonstrating that monitoring accuracy can be trained and that trained monitoring produces learning gains. Phase III draws on this work as evidence that metacognitive calibration is a trainable component.

### From: [[growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11|growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The popular synthesis of Dweck's research program, reaching the broad audience and generating the cultural penetration of the growth mindset concept. Primary source for the conventional account in Phase I.

> [!cite] Reference
> The academic synthesis of Dweck's theoretical framework, providing the most rigorous statement of the implicit theories approach. Essential for understanding the technical distinctions between entity and incremental theories and their proposed effects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing attribution theory's three-dimensional model (locus, stability, controllability) and its predictions for persistence and emotion. The empirical foundation for Axiom 1 of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> Large-scale meta-analysis finding that the relationship between mindset and academic achievement is very small (r ≈ .10), and that mindset interventions produce near-zero effects in general populations with potentially meaningful effects only in at-risk populations. Critical evidence against Assumption 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> The largest preregistered mindset intervention study to date, reporting small but significant effects on challenge-seeking and GPA for lower-achieving students at under-resourced schools. Most favorable recent evidence for targeted mindset effects; used to establish the scope condition for intervention efficacy.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational paper establishing self-efficacy theory and the primacy of mastery experiences as the source of efficacy beliefs. The empirical and theoretical foundation for Axiom 2 of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper establishing the learning/performance goal distinction and demonstrating that goal orientation mediates the relationship between ability beliefs and behavioral responses to failure. Critical foundation for Axiom 3.

> [!cite] Reference
> A systematic methodological critique finding that the evidence base for mindset theory's core causal claims is significantly weaker than typically presented, identifying issues with operationalization, causal inference, and publication bias. Essential methodological context for the assumption audit.

> [!cite] Reference
> The popular synthesis of Dweck's research program, reaching the broad audience and generating the cultural penetration of the growth mindset concept. Primary source for the conventional account in Phase I.

> [!cite] Reference
> The academic synthesis of Dweck's theoretical framework, providing the most rigorous statement of the implicit theories approach. Essential for understanding the technical distinctions between entity and incremental theories and their proposed effects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing attribution theory's three-dimensional model (locus, stability, controllability) and its predictions for persistence and emotion. The empirical foundation for Axiom 1 of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> Large-scale meta-analysis finding that the relationship between mindset and academic achievement is very small (r ≈ .10), and that mindset interventions produce near-zero effects in general populations with potentially meaningful effects only in at-risk populations. Critical evidence against Assumption 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> The largest preregistered mindset intervention study to date, reporting small but significant effects on challenge-seeking and GPA for lower-achieving students at under-resourced schools. Most favorable recent evidence for targeted mindset effects; used to establish the scope condition for intervention efficacy.

### From: [[metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20|metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper introducing the metacognitive knowledge/metacognitive experience distinction that structures this report's Phase II framework. Establishes the two-component model that subsequent researchers, including Nelson and Narens, extend into process-level accounts. Supports Phases II and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces the monitoring-control hierarchical model that is the primary analytical tool of this report. The monitoring-control separation — with its implication that accurate monitoring does not automatically produce appropriate control — is the foundational insight on which this report's analysis of the monitoring-control dissociation in Phase III is built. Supports Phases II, IV, and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Operational specification of the scaffolding construct with six tutoring functions. Introduces the contingency principle and the six functions of scaffolding that remain foundational to instructional design. Supports Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational paper introducing the desirable difficulties framework. The argument that conditions improving long-term retention often slow initial acquisition is central to this report's Phase IV argument that effective metacognitive scaffolding works by making shallow processing harder rather than making learning easier. Supports Phases IV and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Documents the fluency illusion and its systematic decoupling of felt understanding from actual learning. Central to this report's Phase IV mechanistic account of why unscaffolded monitoring is unreliable. Supports Phases III and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Documents the monitoring-without-control phenomenon: delayed summary generation improved monitoring accuracy for poorly understood texts but did not reliably produce increased restudy of those texts. Central evidence for the monitoring-control dissociation argument in Phase III. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates adaptive scaffolding superiority over fixed scaffolding for complex learning tasks. Central evidence for the adaptiveness requirement in Phase III and the expertise reversal implications for Phase V. Supports Phases III and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Documents the expertise reversal effect for instructional support. Important boundary condition for metacognitive scaffolding effectiveness — the support that benefits novices can harm experts. Supports Phases III and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Systematic treatment of productive failure — conditions under which allowing learners to fail before instruction produces better transfer than instruction-first approaches. Directly relevant to the comfort trap in scaffolding design argument in Phase IV. Supports Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Classic treatment of far transfer, arguing that metacognitive and thinking skills are among the most potentially transferable skills but that transfer is not spontaneous — it requires deliberate design conditions ("low road" and "high road" transfer). Relevant to the transfer-dependence failure mode in Phase V and the transfer encoding in Phase VII. Supports Phases V and VII.

> [!cite] Reference
> Zimmerman's cyclical model of self-regulated learning provides the developmental endpoint that metacognitive scaffolding aims toward — the forethought, performance, and self-reflection cycle. Connects scaffolding design to the SRL framework throughout the report. Supports Phases II, V, and VI.

### From: [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23|pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The central theoretical statement of Pintrich's framework. Establishes the four-area architecture and articulates the claim that motivation and context are regulated domains. Essential reading for understanding the theoretical commitments analyzed in Phases II and IV. Particularly valuable for its extended discussion of goal orientation's interaction with cognitive strategy use.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most concise and complete statement of the 4×4 matrix framework. The 2004 paper is the standard reference for the framework's architecture. Supports Phases II and III directly. Recommended sections: the matrix table (pp. 390–392) and the discussion of context regulation (pp. 397–400).

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical study establishing that metacognitive self-regulation and cognitive strategy use predict academic performance, while self-efficacy and intrinsic goal orientation predict cognitive strategy use. The basis for the mediation claim discussed in Phase III. One of the most-cited papers in educational psychology.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary empirical and theoretical treatment of motivational regulation within Pintrich's framework. Develops the taxonomy of motivational regulation strategies and presents survey evidence for their effects on academic outcomes. Directly supports Phase III's treatment of the motivational regulation evidence base.

> [!cite] Reference
> The technical manual for the most widely used SRL measurement instrument. Essential for understanding how the framework's theoretical architecture has been operationalized. Particularly valuable for its discussion of subscale construction and validation.

> [!cite] Reference
> Zimmerman's definitive statement of his cyclical model, published in the same volume as Pintrich's 2000 paper. The juxtaposition clarifies the theoretical differences between the two approaches. Supports the comparative analysis throughout this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The central SDT paper against which the tension with Pintrich's motivational regulation framework is analyzed. Essential for understanding why high-quality autonomous motivation may not require deliberate motivational regulation. Supports Phase III's tension analysis and Phase VI's synthesis.

> [!cite] Reference
> [[Winne-&-Hadwin's-Metacognitive-Processing-Model|Winne and Hadwin's]] information processing model of SRL provides a useful comparison case — more cognitively detailed than Pintrich, less motivationally comprehensive. The comparison clarifies why Pintrich's motivational area is a theoretical advance over purely cognitive SRL models.

### From: [[pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22|pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The mature theoretical statement of Pintrich's integrative framework, presenting the 4×4 matrix in its most developed form. Introduces the four-area, four-phase structure and argues for the treatment of motivation as a parallel regulatory domain. Supports Phases II, IV, and VI of this analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical study demonstrating that self-efficacy is the strongest motivational predictor of cognitive strategy use and academic performance in a sample of seventh-grade students. Established the self-efficacy-to-strategy empirical foundation. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The technical manual for the MSLQ, documenting its factor structure, reliability, and initial validity evidence. The foundational reference for the instrument as a theory-testing tool. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> A later theoretical synthesis applying the framework specifically to college-level learning contexts. Refines the treatment of motivational beliefs and addresses some of the temporal claims more explicitly than earlier papers. Supports Phases II, IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational theoretical source for [[albert-bandura|Bandura's]] self-efficacy construct as used in Pintrich's framework. The social cognitive theory provides the mechanism account for how self-efficacy beliefs are formed, maintained, and revised across the four sources of efficacy information. Supports Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Zimmerman's parallel theoretical account in the same handbook volume. Comparison with Pintrich's chapter in the same volume makes the architectural differences maximally visible. Supports Phase II analysis of how Pintrich's framework differs from Zimmerman's.

> [!cite] Reference
> The COPES model of self-regulated learning as an information processing sequence. Offers a contrasting theoretical perspective that prioritizes cognitive processing precision over motivational integration. Useful as a structural contrast to Pintrich's approach. Supports Phase II structural comparison.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical paper establishing the relationship between classroom goal structures and student goal orientation adoption. Critical support for the contextual area of Pintrich's framework — specifically, the claim that learners' motivational belief architecture is shaped by the motivational climate of the learning environment. Supports Phases II and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Empirical support for the differentiation of task value components (intrinsic, utility, attainment) and their distinct predictive patterns for choice versus effort. Supports Phase V's claim that task value and self-efficacy are functionally distinct motivational pathways.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational implementation intention paper demonstrating that if-then planning in the forethought phase dramatically improves goal attainment under high-demand conditions. Provides empirical support for the cross-domain structural analogue in Phase VII — the parallel between Pintrich's forethought-architecture claim and Gollwitzer's pre-loading logic.

### From: [[sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19|sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The most complete single statement of SDT's theoretical architecture, including the internalization continuum, the basic needs theory, and the goal content framework. Essential reading for Phase II material on SDT. Directly relevant to the autonomy-need dimension and the motivational quality distinctions throughout.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical statement of Zimmerman's three-phase cyclical model. The primary source for Phase II's Zimmerman profile and Phase III's dimension analysis. Particularly important for the forethought-performance-reflection cycle and its application to deliberate practice.

> [!cite] Reference
> Pintrich's most complete statement of his integrative framework. Provides the 4×4 matrix of regulatory areas and phases. The primary source for the "motivational regulation" component that bridges SDT and SRL.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical statement of the COPES model and trace methodology. Foundation for the metacognitive calibration dimension in Phase III. Particularly important for understanding why the Winne & Hadwin framework provides the most cognitively detailed (and motivationally thin) account of SRL.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive meta-analytic confirmation of the undermining effect. Covers over 100 experimental studies. Provides the primary evidence base for SDT's claim that controlling rewards degrade intrinsic motivation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The field-defining handbook containing canonical statements of all major SRL frameworks. Essential reference for any systematic comparison of SRL theories. Contains chapters by Zimmerman, Pintrich, Winne, and Boekaerts himself.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most accessible single-chapter overview of all five SDT mini-theories. Particularly useful for understanding how the CET, OIT, COT, BPNT, and GCT components articulate. Directly informs Phase II's SDT profile and Phase IV's convergence analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most recent and comprehensive textbook statement of SDT. Covers all six mini-theories, cross-cultural evidence, developmental applications, and the integration with clinical psychology. The primary contemporary reference for SDT.

> [!cite] Reference
> A comprehensive survey of the SRL field in its modern form. Includes chapters on the SDT-SRL interface, on metacognitive models, and on domain-specific applications. Provides the evidence base for Phase V's situational navigation recommendations.

> [!cite] Reference
> Important for the developmental analysis in Phase VI — demonstrates how SDT's account of need-supportive vs. need-thwarting parenting maps onto developmental trajectories in self-regulation that parallel Zimmerman's account of regulatory skill development.

### From: [[silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21|silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the Construction-Integration model of reading. Demonstrates that reading involves two phases — construction (activating a broad associative network) and integration (constraining to coherent representations) — and that the depth of Situation Model construction determines the quality of comprehension. Central to Phases II and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal demonstration of the Self-Explanation Effect. Shows that readers who explain texts to themselves during reading produce dramatically better comprehension and transfer than readers who read without self-explanation. The mechanism — forcing Constructive mode engagement — is central to this analysis's Phase III and IV arguments.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the Matthew Effect in reading. Documents the compounding mechanism by which early reading advantages accumulate and proposes the vocabulary-working memory link central to this analysis's account of why critical analysis development is uneven across readers.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational treatment of comprehension monitoring in reading. Establishes that many readers fail to detect text inconsistencies and that comprehension monitoring can be taught. Directly supports this analysis's fourth mechanism and the Illusion of Knowing argument.

> [!cite] Reference
> A comprehensive treatment of think-aloud protocols during reading, demonstrating the constructive and evaluative operations that skilled readers perform during silent reading. Provides empirical grounding for the inference hierarchy proposed in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original levels-of-processing paper. Establishes that depth of semantic processing — not repetition or effort per se — determines retention and comprehension quality. Foundational to Phase II's framework and the distinction between elaborative and evaluative processing.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper establishing the ICAP Framework used as a central analytical tool in Phase II. Demonstrates that Constructive and Interactive engagement modes produce significantly better learning outcomes than Active and Passive modes, and that the mode of engagement is not determined by the activity but by the cognitive operations the learner performs.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational treatment of Desirable Difficulties. Establishes that processing conditions that feel more effortful produce better retention and transfer, and that fluency is a misleading guide to learning depth. Connected to the Fluency Illusion argument in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive review of eye-tracking data on reading behavior, documenting regression patterns and their relationship to inference generation and complex text processing. Provides empirical grounding for the self-pacing mechanism in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> An important empirical paper on actively open-minded thinking and myside bias. Documents the tendency for motivated reasoning to filter evidence in self-serving ways, directly relevant to Phase V's argument about the confirmation bias ceiling of solo silent reading and the necessity of social discourse.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational working memory model paper, establishing the distinction between the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive. The phonological loop mechanism central to this analysis is rooted in this framework.

### From: [[writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21|writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational theoretical work distinguishing knowledge-telling and knowledge-transforming modes of writing. The central theoretical distinction of this analysis. Grounds Phases II and VI.

> [!cite] Reference
> Seminal articulation of desirable difficulties concept and the relationship between fluency and learning. Grounds Phase IV and V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original self-explanation research demonstrating mechanism and effect size. Grounds Phase III and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Framework for ordering learning activities by productive engagement level. Grounds Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive meta-analytic review of learning technique efficacy. Primary evidentiary foundation for Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Contemporary meta-analysis of writing-to-learn research across disciplines. Grounds Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original transfer-appropriate processing research showing encoding-retrieval match governs accessibility. Grounds Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> The laptop vs. longhand note-taking study that is the analytical entry point for this report. Grounds Phase I.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of retrieval practice / testing effect literature. Primary source for Phase III testing effect claims.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original retrieval practice study showing 50% retention advantage over re-reading. Grounds Phase III quantitative claims.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational cognitive load theory paper. Grounds Phase II framework and Phase IV conditional analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> Accessible review integrating retrieval practice, spaced practice, interleaving, and elaboration for instructional applications. Grounds Phase V practical recommendations.

### From: [[writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21|writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text introducing the knowledge-telling/knowledge-transforming distinction. Essential reading for understanding the architectural difference between reproductive and constructive writing processes. Directly supports the Phase II framework and the Phase VI synthesis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive theoretical statement of generative learning theory. Argues that learning is a function of the generative relationships produced between new and existing knowledge. Supports the theoretical architecture in Phase II and the mechanism analysis in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational self-explanation study showing that students who generate explanations during study dramatically outperform those who do not. Core empirical support for Mechanism 3 in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive synthesis rating summarisation as "low utility" and elaborative interrogation as "moderate utility." Essential evidence for the Phase III argument that common writing strategies underperform their apparent effort.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original theoretical statement of the desirable difficulties framework. Writing from memory and retrieval-writing are specific applications of the broader principle that conditions making encoding harder often make retention better. Supports the mechanism analysis in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational research on the fluency illusion in study monitoring. Directly supports the Phase III argument that learners systematically miscalibrate their learning because they use processing fluency as a proxy for encoding strength.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates that retrieval practice dramatically outperforms elaborate re-study. Writing from memory is a form of retrieval practice. Supports the testing effect mechanism in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational CLT paper. Essential for understanding the expertise interaction limitation in Phase V — generative writing tasks can overload novice working memory, undermining learning.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original generation effect study. Demonstrates that material generated by the learner is better remembered than material read. Core empirical support for Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper on epistemic actions — actions performed to change cognitive state rather than advance a task. Writing functions as an epistemic action when it reveals knowledge gaps; this conceptualisation grounds the Phase VIII connection to [[epistemic-actions]].

### From: [[zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23|zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary theoretical statement of the cyclical model in its mature form. This chapter is the canonical source for the three-phase architecture and the phase-transition mechanism. Essential reading for Phases II and IV of this report; the adaptive/defensive inference distinction is elaborated here with unusual precision.

> [!cite] Reference
> A highly accessible summary of the cyclical model with illustrative examples from across learning domains. Particularly useful for the expert-novice comparisons cited in Phase III. Recommended for readers seeking a shorter entry point into the primary literature before reading the full 2000 Handbook chapter.

> [!cite] Reference
> The dart-throwing study cited in Phase III as direct experimental evidence for the self-reflection-to-forethought transition mechanism. Demonstrates that goal type during performance shapes self-reflection content which shapes subsequent self-efficacy. Supports reading in Phase III before examining other empirical studies.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper for the three-dimensional attribution framework used throughout Phase IV's mechanism analysis. The stability × controllability interaction — which generates the asymmetric risk in self-efficacy updating identified in this report — is theorized here with formal precision.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original attribution retraining study showing that teaching children to attribute failure to insufficient effort (rather than ability) improved subsequent persistence and performance. Cited in Phase IV as the primary attribution retraining evidence supporting the self-reflection-first instructional recommendation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The meta-analytic foundation for formative assessment's effectiveness. This report reinterprets Black and Wiliam's findings through the cyclical SRL lens: formative assessment works because it provides externally scaffolded cycle closure. The mechanism their analysis left unspecified is identified here as the self-reflection phase's attribution and adaptive inference sub-processes.

> [!cite] Reference
> The source of the single-loop/double-loop learning distinction used in the FAR transfer phase. The organizational defensive routines Argyris documented are structurally equivalent to the individual defensive inferences Zimmerman theorized. Recommended for readers interested in the organizational learning implications of Phase VII's transfer analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper for deliberate practice theory. Used in Phase VII to establish the deliberate practice–SRL cycle structural analogue. The cycle-closure mechanism's importance for expertise development is implicit in Ericsson's framework but is made explicit by the comparison with Zimmerman's model.

## Learning Science

### From: [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03|goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]]

> [!cite] Reference
> **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P.** (1990). *A theory of goal setting and task performance*. Prentice-Hall.
> **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P.** (2002). Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation. *American Psychologist, 57*(9), 705–717. https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.57.9.705
> **Latham, G. P.** (2012). *Work motivation: History, theory, research, and practice* (2nd ed.). Sage.

## Learning Science & Educational Psychology

### From: [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10|motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational theoretical piece establishing the storage strength vs. retrieval strength distinction that underpins the desirable difficulties framework. Essential for understanding why conditions that reduce immediate performance can enhance long-term retention. Section 2 and 3 of this report rely on this framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> The landmark experimental demonstration that retrieval practice substantially outperforms re-study over week-long retention intervals. The benchmark empirical study for the testing effect claim made throughout this report. Direct reading recommended for any learner seeking to understand the empirical basis for retrieval practice.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive integration of Self-Determination Theory as of 2000, covering the motivational quality continuum, basic psychological needs, and their implications for various life domains including education. The theoretical foundation for Section 4 and 5 of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive meta-analytic review of the spacing effect literature as of 2006, synthesizing over 300 studies and establishing effect size estimates and moderating factors. Direct reading recommended for quantitative understanding of the spacing effect magnitude.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original study documenting the self-explanation effect: learners who spontaneously generated self-explanations while studying worked examples showed substantially superior learning and transfer than those who did not. Essential background for Section 3.6.

> [!cite] Reference
> The key study examining interleaving benefits for category learning, demonstrating that interleaved practice produces better induction and transfer than blocked practice despite learners rating blocked practice as more effective. Central to the treatment of the interleaving effect in Section 3.4.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive treatment of achievement goals within the self-regulated learning framework. Establishes the empirical and theoretical connections between goal orientation and strategy use that Section 5 builds on. The foundational reference for the SRL architecture employed throughout the latter portion of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The taxonomic framework for transfer of learning that organized the Far Transfer section. Essential for understanding the dimensions along which near and far transfer vary, and why structural principle extraction is the key mechanism of far transfer.

> [!cite] Reference
> A concise, accessible overview of the self-regulated learning cycle from one of the framework's principal architects. Provides the forethought-performance-reflection model that Section 5 employs. Recommended as a direct reading complement to the more technical Pintrich 2000 chapter.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive empirical program establishing the role of academic emotions in self-regulated learning and achievement. Foundational for the Control-Value Theory treatment in Section 4.3.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational theoretical piece establishing the storage strength vs. retrieval strength distinction that underpins the desirable difficulties framework. ## Learning Science / Pkb Design

### From: [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10|strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational source for the [[desirable-difficulties]] framework, proposing that conditions that make learning harder during acquisition often produce superior long-term retention. Primary theoretical reference for Sections 2 and throughout. Essential reading for understanding why all seven strategies in this report feel more effortful than passive alternatives.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive meta-review of learning technique effectiveness, evaluating 10 techniques across criteria of utility for educational settings. Provides the high-utility designations for retrieval practice and distributed practice that anchor this report's strategy selection. Indispensable reference for any evidence-based reading system design.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates that retrieval practice outperforms concept mapping (a supposedly deep processing strategy) on both direct retention and inferential tests. Critical evidence for the testing-effect sections and a key source for the claim that retrieval is not merely assessment but a primary learning mechanism.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive meta-analysis of 317 experiments on spacing effects, establishing the optimal spacing formula and confirming the spacing advantage across virtually every studied domain and population. Primary empirical reference for Section 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> Landmark experimental demonstration of the testing effect using educationally realistic materials (prose passages), showing approximately 50% higher retention after one week for retrieval-practice conditions versus re-study conditions. Primary empirical reference for Section 4.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original documentation of the self-explanation effect in physics problem-solving, showing that the number and quality of self-explanations generated during study is the primary predictor of learning gains. Primary empirical reference for Section 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive theoretical statement of [[self-determination-theory]], including the Basic Psychological Needs model, the internalization continuum, and the distinction between autonomous and controlled motivation. Primary reference for Section 8.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive treatment of the cyclical model of self-regulated learning, including the forethought-performance-self-reflection cycle and the role of self-efficacy, goal-setting, and strategy use in effective self-regulation. Primary reference for Section 10.

> [!cite] Reference
> Original documentation of the [[generation-effect]], demonstrating that generated words are remembered better than read words across a wide range of conditions. Primary empirical reference for Section 3.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational statement of the monitoring-control model of metacognition, distinguishing object-level from meta-level processes and the bidirectional communication flows between them. Primary theoretical reference for Section 7.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates the interleaving advantage for category learning (painting attribution), including the striking paradox that participants rated blocked study as more effective despite inferior outcomes. Primary empirical reference for the interleaving sections of Section 5.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational source for the [[desirable-difficulties]] framework, proposing that conditions that make learning harder during acquisition often produce superior long-term retention. Primary theoretical reference for Sections 2 and throughout. Essential reading for understanding why all seven strategies in this report feel more effortful than passive alternatives.

## Learning Theory

### From: [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03|constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Piaget's systematic account of sensorimotor development in the first two years of life — the foundational text for his theory of schemas, assimilation, accommodation, and the constructed nature of object permanence and spatial understanding. The empirical observations are meticulous and the theoretical framework compelling, even where subsequent research has revised the timeline.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary English-language source for Vygotsky's concept of the zone of proximal development, his analysis of play and development, and his account of the social origins of higher mental functions. Essential reading for any constructivist educator.

> [!cite] Reference
> The influential report of the 1959 Woods Hole conference on science education reform. Introduces the spiral curriculum and the argument that any subject can be taught in intellectually honest form at any developmental level. A foundational text for constructivist curriculum design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text of meaningful learning theory and advance organizer research. Contains Ausubel's famous dictum about prior knowledge and systematic treatment of the conditions under which new material is meaningfully rather than rotely learned.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most accessible systematic account of Glasersfeld's radical constructivism — its epistemological foundations, its relationship to Piaget, and its implications for education. A necessary read for anyone engaging seriously with constructivist epistemology.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text of situated learning theory. Analyzes apprenticeship learning in five ethnographic cases and introduces the concepts of community of practice and legitimate peripheral participation. A profound challenge to decontextualized school learning.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most influential critique of minimal-guidance constructivist pedagogies, grounded in cognitive load theory. Essential reading for understanding the empirical and theoretical constraints on constructivist instructional design.

> [!cite] Reference
> Kapur's synthetic account of the productive failure effect — the finding that pre-instruction problem generation, even when unsuccessful, enhances subsequent learning from explicit instruction. Key empirical grounding for a nuanced constructivist instructional design.

> [!cite] Reference
> Updated review of cognitive load theory research — the most comprehensive recent account of the evidence base for worked examples, expertise reversal, and related effects. Essential for integrating constructivism with cognitive science.

> [!cite] Reference
> Meta-analytic review of feedback research — synthesizes the conditions under which feedback promotes learning. The three-level model (task, process, self-regulation) is one of the most influential frameworks in constructivist assessment design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper of conceptual change theory. Proposes four conditions for conceptual change (dissatisfaction, intelligibility, plausibility, fruitfulness) and applies them to science education. Still the primary theoretical reference for the field three decades later.

> [!cite] Reference
> The classic text establishing the reconstructive nature of memory — the empirical foundation for schema theory's account of memory as active construction rather than passive reproduction. Bartlett's prose-passage experiments showing schema-driven distortion are among the most elegant demonstrations in cognitive psychology.

> [!cite] Reference
> Piaget's systematic account of sensorimotor development in the first two years of life — the foundational text for his theory of schemas, assimilation, accommodation, and the constructed nature of object permanence and spatial understanding. > The foundational text of meaningful learning theory and advance organizer research. Contains Ausubel's famous dictum about prior knowledge and systematic treatment of the conditions under which new material is meaningfully rather than rotely learned.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most accessible systematic account of Glasersfeld's radical constructivism — its epistemological foundations, its relationship to Piaget, and its implications for education. A necessary read for anyone engaging seriously with constructivist epistemology.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text of situated learning theory. Analyzes apprenticeship learning in five ethnographic cases and introduces the concepts of community of practice and legitimate peripheral participation. A profound challenge to decontextualized school learning.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most influential critique of minimal-guidance constructivist pedagogies, grounded in cognitive load theory. Essential reading for understanding the empirical and theoretical constraints on constructivist instructional design.

> [!cite] Reference
> Kapur's synthetic account of the productive failure effect — the finding that pre-instruction problem generation, even when unsuccessful, enhances subsequent learning from explicit instruction. Key empirical grounding for a nuanced constructivist instructional design.

> [!cite] Reference
> Updated review of cognitive load theory research — the most comprehensive recent account of the evidence base for worked examples, expertise reversal, and related effects. Essential for integrating constructivism with cognitive science.

> [!cite] Reference
> Meta-analytic review of feedback research — synthesizes the conditions under which feedback promotes learning. The three-level model (task, process, self-regulation) is one of the most influential frameworks in constructivist assessment design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper of conceptual change theory. Proposes four conditions for conceptual change (dissatisfaction, intelligibility, plausibility, fruitfulness) and applies them to science education. Still the primary theoretical reference for the field three decades later.

> [!cite] Reference
> The classic text establishing the reconstructive nature of memory — the empirical foundation for schema theory's account of memory as active construction rather than passive reproduction. Bartlett's prose-passage experiments showing schema-driven distortion are among the most elegant demonstrations in cognitive psychology.

---

## Learning Science

### From: [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30|elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]]

> [!cite] Reference
> This foundational study established the basic EI paradigm and demonstrated robust effects of why-question elaboration on both intentional and incidental memory. Critically introduces the manipulation of elaboration precision and the comparison of generated versus provided elaborations.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Experiments 1-3 for the core EI demonstration; Experiment 4 for the generation advantage.

> [!cite] Reference
> The pivotal prior knowledge moderator study. Systematically manipulated learner background knowledge and demonstrated that EI's advantage over re-reading collapses when prior knowledge is absent. Essential reading for understanding the mechanism argument.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Experiments 1-3 for the prior knowledge manipulation; General Discussion for the theoretical interpretation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The theoretical foundation from which EI research emerged. Introduces the depth-of-processing framework and the distinction between maintenance and elaborative rehearsal. Essential for understanding why EI is usually described as a "deep processing" strategy — and why that description requires upgrading.
> 
> **Historical Significance:** One of the most cited papers in cognitive psychology; initiated decades of research on encoding processes and replaced the structural models of memory with a processing-oriented framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> The source of the three-level text representation model (surface form, textbase, situation model) that serves as the primary analytical framework in this report. The situation model / textbase distinction is the architectural foundation of the Comprehension Gateway Mechanism.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 3-5 for the theoretical architecture; Chapter 6 for the empirical grounding of the situation model construct.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive statement of Kintsch's construction-integration model. Refines the three-level model extensively and provides the computational/process-level account of how situation models are constructed during reading.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive meta-analytic review of learning strategies. Rates EI as moderate utility, identifies the prior knowledge moderator as a key limitation, and situates EI within the broader strategy landscape. Essential for understanding EI's position relative to retrieval practice, distributed practice, and other high-utility strategies.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Section 3.2 on elaborative interrogation; the General Discussion for practical implications.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational generation effect paper. Demonstrates that self-generated items are remembered better than read items across a variety of paradigms. Provides the theoretical backdrop for the EI generation advantage discussed in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical statement of the desirable difficulties framework. Establishes that conditions that impair performance during acquisition often enhance long-term retention — the theoretical context in which EI's cognitive demand is understood as beneficial rather than merely taxing.

> [!cite] Reference
> Extension of the EI research program to older children. Documents the generation advantage and provides evidence that EI's mechanism requires the active construction of causal connections, not merely the provision of additional information about the topic.

> [!cite] Reference
> Directly demonstrates that EI effect magnitude varies with question type — a finding that presages the Dual Register Diagnostic. Questions requiring genuine causal reasoning produce larger effects than questions that can be answered by paraphrase or text retrieval.

### From: [[vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28|vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing depth of processing as the primary determinant of memory durability. Proposed that "depth" corresponds to the degree of semantic-elaborative processing — a claim that directly grounds the report's account of why incidental vocabulary encounters produce shallow traces. Required reading for understanding the mechanism-level argument.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The original processing levels taxonomy; the experimental evidence for depth effects on retention.
> 
> **Historical Significance:** Challenged the dominant structural (multi-store) memory models of the 1960s by showing that the *process* of encoding, not the *store* it entered, determined durability.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal paper introducing the Matthew Effect to reading research. Synthesises developmental, cognitive, and sociological evidence to argue that early literacy differences compound into widening gaps. This report's spiral model extends Stanovich's account by specifying the cognitive load and context quality mechanisms that drive the compounding.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The "rich get richer" developmental trajectory evidence; the print exposure data; the implications for instructional policy.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive treatment of vocabulary acquisition research available. Covers acquisition rates, coverage ratios, word frequency lists, and instructional approaches. The 10–15 encounter estimate and the 95–98% coverage threshold evidence drawn in this report come from this source.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapter 3 (learning from context); Chapter 4 (deliberate vocabulary learning); the word frequency and coverage ratio data in the appendices.

> [!cite] Reference
> One of the earliest systematic empirical studies of incidental vocabulary acquisition rates, tracking word knowledge gains in fifth-grade readers. Established the 5–15% per-encounter acquisition rate estimate that has been widely replicated.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The methodology section (for understanding what the acquisition measures actually captured); the discussion of moderating variables.

> [!cite] Reference
> The empirical foundation for the vocabulary coverage threshold claim. Demonstrates that comprehension of academic English texts requires coverage ratios approaching 95% and links coverage deficits to specific comprehension failure patterns.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational text establishing verbal efficiency theory. The argument that lexical representation quality determines working memory availability for higher-order comprehension — the mechanism that drives the spiral model's positive trajectory — is developed here in full.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most practical treatment of vocabulary instruction research, synthesising evidence for the conditions under which direct instruction produces robust, transferable word knowledge. Directly relevant to the report's claim that direct instruction is a spiral-priming mechanism.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The tiered vocabulary framework; the "robust instruction" principles; the evidence for depth-of-processing effects in direct instruction.

> [!cite] Reference
> Empirical test of the involvement load hypothesis, which extends the levels-of-processing account to specify which vocabulary tasks produce the deepest encoding. Supports the depth-primary view of acquisition with direct experimental evidence.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive statement of the comprehensible input hypothesis — that acquisition is automatic given input at the right difficulty level (+i). This report's mechanistic account challenges Krashen's automaticity assumption while accepting the difficulty-band insight.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational text for [[dual-coding-theory]]. The prediction that words encoded with both verbal and imagistic representations will be more robustly acquired grounds the practical recommendation for elaborative inference during reading.

### From: [[zimmerman-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22|zimmerman-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the SRL model within Bandura's Social Cognitive Theory framework. Zimmerman articulates the three phases and their interrelations for the first time. Supports Phases II and IV throughout.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive chapter-length treatment of the three-phase cyclical model. Provides the most complete account of Self-Reflection Phase components (self-judgment and self-reaction). Foundational for the entire analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational self-efficacy paper. Establishes the four sources of efficacy information and the prospective, domain-specific nature of self-efficacy beliefs. Supports the self-efficacy feedback loop analysis in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Weiner's comprehensive attributional framework, establishing the locus-controllability dimensions. The theoretical foundation for the Attributional Bridge construct. Supports Phase III and Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes the attribution-learned helplessness link and demonstrates the asymmetric development of maladaptive attribution patterns. Evidence for the asymmetric ratchet construct in Phase VI.

> [!cite] Reference
> Path analysis demonstrating that self-efficacy mediates the performance-outcome to subsequent performance link through attribution. Direct evidence for the attribution-mediation finding in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates that mastery-standard evaluation produces better self-efficacy, engagement, and learning outcomes than normative evaluation. Supports Phase III analysis of evaluative standards.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes the goal orientation moderation of attributional tendencies — mastery goal orientation predicts effort/strategy attribution, performance orientation predicts ability attribution. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Articulates the three types of metacognitive knowledge (declarative, procedural, conditional) and their developmental patterns. Foundation for the conditional knowledge-attribution quality link in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes that academic emotions are determined by perceived control and task value, and that they influence cognitive processing including attributional tendencies. Supports the emotional mediation limitation noted in Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Provides the theoretical foundation for understanding defensive inference as psychologically functional in contexts where performance is used as a signal of self-worth. Supports the tension-identified callout in Phase IV.

## Metacognition

### From: [[pkb-as-externalized-metacognition-foundational-report-2026-04-12|pkb-as-externalized-metacognition-foundational-report-2026-04-12]]

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational work on desirable difficulties — the principle that certain kinds of cognitive effort enhance long-term learning even when they reduce performance during training. Relevant to this report's argument that productive friction in PKB design supports metacognitive monitoring. The chapter also links memory research to metamemory, establishing the connection between learning efficiency and metacognitive accuracy.

> [!cite] Reference
> Seminal study demonstrating the self-explanation effect — students who explain material to themselves during learning achieve significantly deeper understanding. Relevant to this report's argument that written self-explanation in a PKB provides metacognitive monitoring by forcing articulation of reasoning.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper for Extended Mind Theory, introducing the Parity Principle and the Otto/Inga thought experiment. Provides the philosophical justification for this report's central claim that PKBs can constitute part of the user's metacognitive architecture rather than merely influencing it externally.

> [!cite] Reference
> Flavell's definitive articulation of the metacognitive framework, distinguishing metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy) from metacognitive experience. Provides the component architecture that this report maps onto PKB features in the Metacognitive Architecture Mapping Framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foucault's analysis of ancient hypomnemata as technologies of the self — practices of writing used for ethical self-examination and personal transformation. Establishes the historical precedent for externalized metacognition and situates modern PKB practice within a 2,000-year intellectual lineage.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces the distinction between epistemic actions (performed to change one's informational relationship to a problem) and pragmatic actions (performed to achieve a goal state). Provides the conceptual vocabulary for understanding PKB writing as an extended epistemic action.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive statement of the meta-level/object-level framework for metacognition. Provides the structural vocabulary used throughout this report — particularly the monitoring/control distinction and the meta-level/object-level architecture that the PKB externalizes.

> [!cite] Reference
> Classic paper demonstrating that people lack accurate introspective access to their own higher-order cognitive processes. Provides the foundational evidence for Section I's argument about the opacity of thought and the Cognitive Transparency Assumption.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of research on cognitive offloading — the strategic use of external tools to reduce internal cognitive demands. Relevant to this report's discussion of the relationship between offloading and externalized metacognition, and to the scaffold dependence concern.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces the knowledge-telling/knowledge-transforming distinction that is central to this report's analysis of writing as metacognitive technology. Establishes that writing can be either passive transcription or active knowledge construction depending on the cognitive processes engaged.

> [!cite] Reference
> Develops the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory (MAI) measuring knowledge about cognition and regulation of cognition. Provides empirical operationalization of the metacognitive components discussed throughout this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> Overview of the cyclical model of self-regulated learning (forethought → performance → self-reflection). Relevant to this report's discussion of how PKB templates and workflows externalize the SRL cycle.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational work on desirable difficulties — the principle that certain kinds of cognitive effort enhance long-term learning even when they reduce performance during training. Relevant to this report's argument that productive friction in PKB design supports metacognitive monitoring. The chapter also links memory research to metamemory, establishing the connection between learning efficiency and metacognitive accuracy.

> [!cite] Reference
> Seminal study demonstrating the self-explanation effect — students who explain material to themselves during learning achieve significantly deeper understanding. Relevant to this report's argument that written self-explanation in a PKB provides metacognitive monitoring by forcing articulation of reasoning.

## Motivation Science

### From: [[inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25|inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper for the three-dimensional attribution model (locus, stability, controllability) and their emotional and motivational consequences. Phases II, III, and IV of this report draw directly on this dimensional architecture. Recommended for anyone wanting the empirical foundation for the attribution vocabulary tools.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive synthesis of self-efficacy theory, including the four-sources model and the verbal persuasion source. The treatment of verbal persuasion (Chapter 4) is the theoretical basis for this report's extension of the concept to inner speech. Chapters 3 and 4 most relevant to the self-efficacy calibration mechanism.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational SDT text. The Cognitive Evaluation Theory section establishes the controlled-autonomous regulatory language distinction. The internalization framework (Chapter 4) provides the theoretical basis for the authenticity tension analysis in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Dweck's comprehensive treatment of implicit theories of intelligence, including the praise vocabulary research and its effects on motivational fragility vs. resilience. Chapter 2 on the consequences of ability vs. process praise is directly relevant to the Motivational Lexicon synthesis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical statement of Zimmerman's Cyclical SRL Model. The three-phase architecture (Forethought, Performance, Self-Reflection) provides the structural framework for the Cross-Phase Vocabulary Cascade mechanism.

> [!cite] Reference
> The empirical foundation for the Achievement Goal Theory framework used in Phase III. The mastery-approach/mastery-avoidance/performance-approach/performance-avoidance distinction provides the goal orientation vocabulary framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> The key study establishing that regulatory language framing (autonomous vs. controlled) of identical tasks produces different engagement depth and transfer quality. The extension to inner speech regulatory vocabulary in this report builds on this evidence.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational theoretical account of inner speech as internalized social speech. Provides the developmental and functional framework for understanding why inner speech vocabulary carries motivational force — the vocabulary was originally social before becoming private.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive review of goal-setting theory, including the specificity and challenge principles that underlie the Goal Construction Vocabulary Template (Tool 4). The review of 35 years of goal-setting research is the empirical foundation for goal vocabulary recommendations.

> [!cite] Reference
> The reformulated learned helplessness model that maps Weiner's attribution dimensions onto the helplessness-resilience continuum. Provides the empirical evidence that stable-internal-uncontrollable attributional vocabulary produces [[learned-helplessness]] outcomes — the most destructive end-state of the Attributional Vocabulary Loop.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper on implementation intentions (if-then planning). The evidence that specific regulatory language ("When I am in X, I will do Y") dramatically outperforms vague motivational language ("I will try") provides the FAR Transfer evidence for the application bridge to habit formation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational emotional intelligence framework. The connection between emotional vocabulary richness and emotion regulation precision provides the structural analogue for the Motivational Lexicon's claim that vocabulary is a regulatory resource.

---

## Motivational Psychology

### From: [[attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03|attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]]

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Foundational Theory | **Significance:** The origin point of Attribution Theory. Introduced naive psychology, the personal/environmental causality distinction, and the foundational framework for all subsequent attribution research.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Theoretical Development | **Significance:** First formal presentation of the ANOVA covariation model, establishing the consensus/distinctiveness/consistency analysis framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Theoretical Synthesis | **Significance:** The definitive statement of Weiner's dimensional model and its application to motivation and emotion. The core reference for educational applications of Attribution Theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Foundational Theory | **Significance:** Correspondent inference theory; the formal account of when observers make dispositional attributions.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Applied Theory | **Significance:** Identification and naming of the Fundamental Attribution Error; systematic analysis of dispositional attribution biases.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Clinical Application | **Significance:** The reformulated learned helplessness model introducing attributional style as the mechanism of depressive helplessness. Foundation of cognitive vulnerability-stress models of depression.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Educational Application | **Significance:** Classic experimental demonstration of attribution retraining effects on learned helplessness in children; the foundational study for the mastery/helpless response pattern distinction.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Educational Application | **Significance:** Detailed qualitative and quantitative analysis of cognitive-motivational differences between mastery-oriented and helpless children after failure; documented the attribution-thought-strategy chain.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Intervention Research | **Significance:** Pioneering experimental demonstration of attribution retraining with college freshmen; GPA improvement and reduced dropout through stability reframing.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Cross-Cultural | **Significance:** Landmark developmental-cross-cultural study demonstrating cultural divergence in dispositional vs. situational attribution tendencies across childhood — the foundational evidence that the FAE is culturally modulated.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Intervention Research | **Significance:** High-impact demonstration of attributional belonging intervention effects on GPA and health across three years; particularly large effects for African-American students; widely cited in educational equity literature.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Category:** Developmental | **Significance:** Formal developmental account of the differentiation of ability and effort conceptions; the Stage 1-2-3 developmental sequence; the foundation of developmental trajectory work in Attribution Theory.

### From: [[basic-psychological-needs-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01|basic-psychological-needs-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Deci, E. L. (1971). Effects of externally mediated rewards on intrinsic motivation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 18*(1), 105–115. [The original undermining effect experiment — nucleus of BPNT]
> - Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (1985). *Intrinsic motivation and self-determination in human behavior*. Plenum. [First full articulation of SDT and the need framework]
> - Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (2000). The "what" and "why" of goal pursuits: Human needs and the self-determination of behavior. *Psychological Inquiry, 11*(4), 227–268. [Comprehensive BPNT statement and theoretical elaboration]
> - Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2000). Self-determination theory and the facilitation of intrinsic motivation, social development, and well-being. *American Psychologist, 55*(1), 68–78. [Most-cited SDT overview]
> - Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2017). *Self-determination theory: Basic psychological needs in motivation, development, and wellness*. Guilford Press. [Definitive comprehensive treatment of the full SDT framework including BPNT]

> [!cite] Reference
> - Vansteenkiste, M., & Ryan, R. M. (2013). ## Motivational Psychology

### From: [[achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24|achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational theoretical paper establishing the distinction between task and ego involvement and the differentiated ability concept. Essential for understanding the historical preconditions of the 2×2 framework. Recommended in full — Nicholls' theoretical exposition is unusually clear. Primary source for Phase II.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that established the 2×2 framework in its current form. Provides the theoretical rationale for adding the approach-avoidance dimension, the initial validation of the four-cell measurement structure, and evidence for the differential outcome associations. Primary source for Phases II and III.

> [!cite] Reference
> A theoretical review paper that articulates the hierarchical model connecting personality-level approach/avoidance motivation to achievement goal adoption. Provides mechanistic grounding for the approach-avoidance asymmetry. Recommended for Phase IV background.

> [!cite] Reference
> The theoretical integration paper connecting implicit theories of intelligence to goal orientation and behavioral response patterns. Establishes the failure attribution mechanism and the helpless vs. mastery-oriented response profiles. Primary source for Phase IV's attribution discussion.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive meta-analysis partially resolving the PAP goals controversy. Essential for understanding the context-dependence of PAP effects and the measurement issues underlying the debate. Recommended for Phase III evidence evaluation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the TARGET framework for motivational climate design. Provides the environmental complement to the individual-level goal orientation analysis. Primary source for Phase V motivational climate discussion.

> [!cite] Reference
> The empirical investigation of the help-seeking mechanism connecting achievement goals to adaptive learning behavior. Primary source for the Phase IV help-seeking mechanism discussion.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper on regulatory focus theory — the most theoretically elaborated framework for understanding approach-avoidance regulation in human motivation. The FAR Transfer discussion in Phase VII draws on this work.

### From: [[attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24|attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal theoretical paper that formally articulated the three-dimensional model and the emotion-mediation framework. Phase IV's mechanism analysis draws directly on this formulation. The paper is essential reading for understanding how Weiner integrated motivation and emotion into a single theoretical architecture.

> [!cite] Reference
> The book-length development of the 1985 paper, including the social attribution applications and the controllability-moral inference material analyzed in Phase IV. The most complete statement of Weiner's theoretical framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper connecting Weiner's stability dimension to clinical depression and learned helplessness. The reformulated learned helplessness model is the primary evidence base for the stability-expectancy mechanism's clinical significance.

> [!cite] Reference
> Lazarus's comprehensive statement of appraisal theory. The structural correspondence with Weiner's attribution dimensions, analyzed in Phase IV, is most clearly visible when reading Lazarus alongside Weiner. Critical for the intervention implications explored in Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that established the entity/incremental implicit theory distinction and connected it to helpless and mastery response patterns. The direct operationalization of Weiner's stability dimension as an individual difference variable.

> [!cite] Reference
> Weiner's application of controllability attribution to social cognition — specifically the controllability-helping-anger analysis reviewed in Phase III. Essential for understanding how Attribution Theory functions as a theory of moral cognition.

> [!cite] Reference
> An important applied study demonstrating the controllability-helping pathway in racially charged real-world contexts. Supports the external validity of the laboratory findings reviewed in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational attribution retraining study demonstrating that shifting stability attributions for academic difficulty improves subsequent performance. Directly relevant to Phase V's discussion of intervention design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The Affect Infusion Model, documenting that mood states systematically influence causal attributions. The primary evidence base for the bidirectionality tension identified in Phase III.

### From: [[self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11|self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical study demonstrating the undermining effect of external rewards on intrinsic motivation. Launched the SDT research programme by challenging behaviourist orthodoxy and establishing the importance of perceived locus of causality.

> [!cite] Reference
> The first comprehensive theoretical statement of SDT, integrating CET, OIT, and the basic needs framework. Establishes the organismic-integration philosophical architecture and the three-need structure.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive meta-analysis of the undermining effect, examining 128 studies. Finds that expected, tangible, contingent rewards undermine intrinsic motivation; unexpected, non-contingent, verbal rewards do not. Highly influential; also the subject of significant methodological debate (see Cameron & Pierce).

> [!cite] Reference
> The most-cited summary of SDT, accessible overview of the six mini-theories and key empirical findings. The best entry point for understanding the conventional SDT framework as intended by its architects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper distinguishing independent and interdependent self-construals and their implications for motivation, cognition, and affect. Essential context for evaluating SDT's cross-cultural universality claims, particularly regarding the autonomy need.

> [!cite] Reference
> Develops the distinction between need satisfaction and need frustration as partially independent dimensions — a significant internal revision of the original SDT model. Demonstrates that need frustration predicts ill-being beyond what need satisfaction alone explains.

> [!cite] Reference
> Proposes beneficence as a fourth candidate basic psychological need, independent of the three SDT needs, with empirical evidence for its contribution to well-being. Challenges the completeness claim of the three-need structure.

> [!cite] Reference
> Philosophical analysis of the concept of innateness and the evidential standards required to establish that a cognitive or motivational property is biologically grounded rather than universally acquired. Provides the philosophical framework for evaluating SDT's innateness claim rigorously.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal paper in the philosophy of agency distinguishing first-order desires from second-order volitions and locating freedom of will in the alignment between these levels. Provides the philosophical benchmark against which SDT's autonomy construct can be measured — and found to fall short of full self-determination.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text of attachment theory, establishing that affiliative bonds are a biological system that shapes developmental trajectories and internal working models. Provides the theoretical grounding for Axiom 4's claim that secure attachment is a precondition for autonomous motivation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical study demonstrating the undermining effect of external rewards on intrinsic motivation. Launched the SDT research programme by challenging behaviourist orthodoxy and establishing the importance of perceived locus of causality.

> [!cite] Reference
