### From: [[self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11|self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text in the SDL field. Knowles articulates the concept of andragogy and develops its implications for SDL practice, including the learning contract methodology. Essential reading for understanding the historical roots of the field and the process model that remains the dominant reference framework. Readers should approach it with awareness that Knowles writes primarily from a practitioner perspective; the theoretical foundations are underdeveloped by contemporary standards, but the pedagogical vision is clear and still influential.

> [!cite] Reference
> The empirical cornerstone of the SDL literature. Tough's large-scale interview study documenting the prevalence, intentionality, and self-planned character of naturally occurring adult learning established that SDL is descriptive as well as normative. Essential reading for Phase II and Phase IV; the methodology (the detailed interview protocol used to surface learning projects) is directly relevant to the design of SDL support tools.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original development and validation study for the SDLRS. Should be read alongside later psychometric critiques (particularly Bonham, 1991, and Field, 1989) for a balanced view of the instrument's validity. Guglielmino's conceptualisation of self-directedness as a multi-faceted readiness construct, rather than a simple attitude, remains theoretically significant even where the specific factor structure is disputed.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most theoretically sophisticated and philosophically grounded treatment of SDL in the literature. Candy's constructivist account, his four-fold conceptual distinction, and his historical survey of the field remain the standard reference for scholars seeking rigorous engagement with the construct. The book is long and dense, but the opening conceptual chapters repay careful reading. Directly supports Phases II and III of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The source of the Personal Responsibility Orientation (PRO) model. Essential for understanding the process-person distinction that resolves the field's major definitional ambiguity. The authors also provide a comprehensive review of research to that point and a critical analysis of where the SDL field needed to go; their predictions about the field's development proved largely accurate.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper introducing the three-component SDL model integrating self-management, self-monitoring, and motivation. One of the most theoretically sophisticated and empirically grounded treatments in the literature. Directly supports Phase III and Phase IV. Essential reading for anyone who wants to connect SDL theory to cognitive psychology and motivational research.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original articulation of heutagogy as an extension of SDL. Short and accessible, though the theoretical foundations are sketched rather than developed. Should be read alongside Hase and Kenyon's 2007 elaboration for a more complete account. Important for connecting SDL theory to capability development and to the challenge of learning for complex, unpredictable environments.

> [!cite] Reference
> An early and still useful critical engagement with SDL that raises ideological questions about the autonomy discourse. Brookfield's subsequent work develops these critiques further. Essential reading for avoiding uncritical acceptance of SDL rhetoric and for understanding the political dimensions of apparently neutral pedagogical choices. Directly supports the critical section of Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational statement of Self-Determination Theory. While not primarily about SDL, this paper provides the theoretical foundation for SDL's motivational mechanisms — specifically the distinction between intrinsic motivation, identified regulation, and external regulation, and their differential effects on learning quality and persistence. Essential background for understanding why not all self-directed learning is equally effective.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most influential statement of Self-Regulated Learning theory from a social cognitive perspective. Essential for understanding the distinction between SDL and SRL and for mapping the conceptual landscape around learner autonomy. Supports Phase III's discussion of the SDL-SRL distinction and Phase IV's metacognitive mechanisms section.

### From: [[self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06|self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The authoritative statement of Zimmerman's cyclical model, detailing the three-phase architecture and the role of self-motivational beliefs. Essential reading for the forethought phase mechanisms analyzed in Phases II and IV of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> Provides experimental evidence for the process-goal shift mechanism and the mediating role of self-efficacy. Directly supports the efficacy-calibration analysis in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Pintrich's definitive statement on motivational integration in SRL, distinguishing goal orientation types and their differential effects on regulatory behavior. Essential for Phase IV's goal orientation analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary validation study for the MSLQ. The limitations of self-report methodology discussed in Phase III require familiarity with this instrument's design and the assumptions embedded in it.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper distinguishing metacognitive knowledge from metacognitive experiences. The theoretical architecture of this distinction underpins the monitoring analysis throughout Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Bandura's comprehensive treatment of self-efficacy theory, including its sources, calibration, and effects on behavior. The dynamic recalibration of efficacy analyzed in Phase IV requires familiarity with chapters on efficacy appraisal processes.

> [!cite] Reference
> Meta-analysis of 74 intervention studies. The finding of high variance in effect sizes (the central puzzle motivating this analysis) is documented here, along with the moderating role of targeting metacognitive monitoring specifically.

> [!cite] Reference
> Influential analysis of feedback research identifying self-regulation feedback as the most potent feedback level. The distinction between feedback that does regulatory work for the learner versus feedback that builds regulatory capacity directly informs Phase V's intervention implications.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces trace methodology and the information-processing model of SRL. The argument that self-monitoring is hypothesis testing rather than passive surveillance is developed here and directly informs the Phase IV mechanism analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> Documents the expertise reversal effect that moderates SRL intervention effectiveness at different stages of domain knowledge development. Essential for the Phase III critique of universal SRL intervention application.

> [!cite] Reference
> Dweck's framework on implicit theories of intelligence and their effects on attribution patterns following failure. The connection to SRL's self-reflection phase failure modes analyzed in Phase IV is direct and mechanistically specified.

### From: [[situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29|situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational source for the three-level model of text representation and the original statement of the situation model construct. Directly supports Phase II's framework section and provides the theoretical basis for the challenges in Phase III. The introduction and Chapter 1 are the most relevant sections for this report's argument.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary statement of the Construction-Integration model. Central to Phase IV's alternative framework treatment. This paper provides the mechanistic account of why integration fails and why prior knowledge is constitutive rather than merely facilitative of comprehension.

> [!cite] Reference
> The full elaboration of the CI model with comprehensive empirical review. The most authoritative synthesis of the alternative framework offered in Phase IV. Chapter 4 on situation models and Chapter 6 on individual differences are most directly relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original statement of the Event Indexing Model. Provides the empirical basis for the five-dimension structure of situation models; supports the monitoring checklist recommendations in Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of situation model research. Directly supports Phase III's empirical challenge and Phase IV's alternative framework. The review's treatment of the five dimensions and their empirical evidence is the primary source for the Event Indexing Model discussion.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original statement of the Simple View of Reading. Central to the steel-man construction in Phase I. Should be read to understand the best version of the received view's empirical support before engaging with the challenges.

> [!cite] Reference
> Key empirical source for the specific comprehender dissociation. Central to Phase III's empirical challenge. Demonstrates the dissociability of decoding and situation model construction in children.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original Matthew Effect paper. Supports Phase III's critical lens and Phase VII's PKB connections discussion. Explains the compounding nature of situation model deficits over developmental time.

> [!cite] Reference
> A comprehensive review of the inference generation literature. Supports Phase III's empirical challenge regarding the non-automatic nature of elaborative inferences. The review's treatment of inference types and their generation conditions is the primary source for the inference discussion.

> [!cite] Reference
> The baseball knowledge study. Direct empirical support for the prior-knowledge-as-constitutive claim in Phase III. Demonstrates that topic knowledge overrides verbal ability as a predictor of situation model quality.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational source for schema theory. Supports the philosophical challenge in Phase III regarding text-self-sufficiency; demonstrates experimentally that comprehension and memory are reconstructive processes that depend on schemas, not extractive processes that depend on texts. [[frederic-bartlett]]

### From: [[situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29|situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The founding monograph of the situation model framework. Introduces the three-level representation architecture, the textbase-situation model distinction, macrostructure, and the local/global coherence distinction. Required primary reading for anyone working in discourse comprehension.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 2 (propositional analysis), 5 (situation models), 8 (comprehension strategies).

> [!cite] Reference
> Kintsch's comprehensive synthesis of 30 years of research on text comprehension, integrating propositional analysis, the Construction-Integration model, and extensions to learning from text and problem solving. The definitive C-I model account.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 1 (overview), 4 (C-I model), 6 (situation models and knowledge), 10 (learning from text).

> [!cite] Reference
> The original Event Indexing Model paper. Concise but theoretically dense; provides the structural specification of situation model content across five dimensions.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of the situation model literature through the late 1990s. The most accessible survey of the empirical evidence base for the situation model as a distinct level of representation.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original formal presentation of the C-I model. More accessible than the 1998 book; the clearest statement of the two-phase architecture and its psychological motivations.

> [!cite] Reference
> Presents the Structure Building Framework with empirical support, including the suppression mechanism and individual differences evidence. The most direct treatment of why comprehension skill is partly determined by inhibitory capacity.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive statement of Constructionist Theory. Specifies which inference types are generated automatically (causal antecedents, goal-motives) versus selectively (predictive, elaborative).

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational study establishing the correlation between reading span (WMC measure) and reading comprehension. First rigorous demonstration that WMC constraints affect situation model construction quality.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal self-explanation study. Demonstrates that prompting readers to explain content produces substantially better comprehension (including more inference questions answered correctly) than re-reading. Primary empirical support for self-explanation as a situation model construction strategy.

> [!cite] Reference
> Presents the Landscape Model and the standards of coherence concept. Explains how individual differences in coherence standards produce different rates of inference generation and different situation model qualities.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational study demonstrating that causal connectivity — the number of causal connections a text event has — is the primary predictor of recall probability, more predictive than serial position. The empirical basis for the primacy of causal structure in situation model construction.

> [!cite] Reference
> Counterintuitive finding: high-knowledge readers learn more from low-coherence texts (they must generate more inferences, producing deeper processing); low-knowledge readers learn more from high-coherence texts (explicit coherence compensates for absent prior knowledge). Demonstrates the interaction between domain knowledge and text coherence in situation model construction.

> [!cite] Reference
> Classic demonstration of prior knowledge dependence in comprehension. The "washing clothes" passage: incomprehensible without context; highly comprehensible with it. Among the most elegant demonstrations of the schema-dependence of situation model construction.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces the propositional analysis methodology and the macrostructure model. Essential historical context for understanding how the situation model framework emerged from the propositional tradition.

### From: [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24|srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary theoretical source for the three-phase cyclical model analyzed throughout this report. Essential reading for anyone implementing the Forethought/Self-Reflection framework. Particularly valuable for its detailed explication of the two-subsystem architecture of each phase.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational treatment of self-efficacy theory. The four sources of self-efficacy discussed in Part II and the mastery experience architecture underlying the motivational design in Part VIII are grounded in this work. Chapters 3 and 8 are most directly applicable.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical paper for the three-dimensional attribution analysis developed in Part IV. Essential for the Zone 3 attribution protocol in Template 2. The locus-stability-controllability framework is articulated here in its most rigorous form.

> [!cite] Reference
> The defining paper for the mastery/performance goal orientation distinction as applied to educational settings. Grounds the language bank in Template 4 and the motivational architecture in Part VIII. Directly applicable to the design of self-directed learning environments.

> [!cite] Reference
> Dweck's foundational articulation of entity vs. incremental theories of intelligence and their relationship to achievement goal orientations. The implicit theory → attribution → self-reaction chain described in Part IV is developed most clearly here.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive empirical demonstration of the self-explanation effect that grounds the Cold Reconstruction protocol and the Self-Explanation Scaffold in Template 6. The mechanism — self-explanation forces integration of new information with prior knowledge — is directly applicable to PKB-integrated reading.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational treatment of desirable difficulties and the fluency illusion. Essential context for understanding why the Cold Reconstruction protocol's difficulty is a feature rather than a bug, and why defensive inferences toward easier reading conditions are typically miscalibrated.

> [!cite] Reference
> The landmark meta-analysis on formative assessment. While focused on classroom contexts, the principles directly apply to self-directed learning: specific behavioral feedback (not global evaluations), process-focused criteria, and the critical role of clear learning intentions established before instruction. The PKB Forethought protocol's goal-setting design reflects these principles.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that established metacognition as a major research field. The declarative/procedural/conditional distinction developed in Part VII derives from this framework. Essential for understanding the metacognitive substrate of both Forethought and Self-Reflection.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational deliberate practice paper. The SRL cycle's feedback loop — as the mechanism by which self-directed reading develops toward expertise — structurally parallels Ericsson's deliberate practice architecture. The connection between these two frameworks is one of the most productive cross-domain links in the PKB.

### From: [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24|srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The chapter that provides the most comprehensive account of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model, including the sub-processes of both the Forethought and Self-Reflection phases that this report operationalises. Essential reading for the theoretical grounding of every template in Phase VII.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The sections on Forethought Phase components (pp. 16–22) and Self-Reflection Phase components (pp. 28–35) directly ground this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The key empirical study demonstrating process-goal superiority during skill acquisition phases, with the developmental trajectory toward outcome goals as expertise develops. The process goal / outcome goal distinction in the Forethought Protocol derives directly from this research.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Study 1 (process vs. outcome goal conditions) and the discussion of developmental phase implications.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive treatment of self-efficacy theory, including the four sources of efficacy information, the measurement challenges, and the educational applications. Grounds every component of the self-efficacy check in the Forethought Protocol.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 3 (Sources of Self-Efficacy) and 6 (Academic Functioning) are most directly relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the object-level/meta-level architecture and the monitoring/control distinction that structures Phase V's metacognitive analysis. The calibration concept and the monitoring-control distinction both derive from this paper.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The theoretical framework section (pp. 125–140) and the empirical findings on monitoring accuracy (pp. 140–160).

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper establishing the 2×2 achievement goal framework (mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, performance-avoidance) that grounds the goal orientation discussions in Phases II and VI. The mastery-grammar design principle derives from the implications of this framework.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The conceptual differentiation between approach and avoidance within mastery orientation (pp. 502–506) and the predictive validity findings (pp. 509–515).

> [!cite] Reference
> The landmark meta-analysis establishing the principles of formative assessment and their relationship to learning quality. The descriptive vs. evaluative feedback distinction, and the learning-gap framework, ground the prompt design principles in Phase VI.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Sections on feedback (pp. 53–64) and the distinction between formative and summative assessment functions.

> [!cite] Reference
> The comprehensive theoretical statement of Self-Determination Theory, establishing autonomy, competence, and relatedness as basic psychological needs and their implications for intrinsic motivation. Grounds the SDT lens in Phase VI and the autonomy-supportive language guide.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The theoretical overview (pp. 227–240) and the educational applications section (pp. 254–262).

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing self-compassion as distinct from self-esteem and demonstrating its relationship to psychological wellbeing and resilience. Grounds the self-compassion discussion in Phase VI and the emotional close component of the Self-Reflection Template.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The conceptual differentiation from self-esteem (pp. 85–92) and the components of self-compassion (pp. 87–90).

### From: [[stoicism-codebase-pack|stoicism-codebase-pack]]

> [!cite] Reference
> **Primary Sources and Scholarly Works**
> 
> The research for this report synthesized information from multiple authoritative sources on ancient Stoicism and Hellenistic philosophy:
> 
> - [Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Stoicism](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/stoicism/) — Comprehensive academic overview of Stoic philosophy, its historical development, and core doctrines
> - [Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy: Stoicism](https://iep.utm.edu/stoicism/) — Detailed treatment of Stoic metaphysics, epistemology, and ethics
> - [Ancient History Encyclopedia: Stoicism](https://www.worldhistory.org/stoicism/) — Historical context and biographical information on major Stoic figures
> - [Stanford Encyclopedia: Hellenistic Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/hellenistic-philosophy/) — Broader context of Hellenistic philosophical movements
> - [Stanford Encyclopedia: Epictetus](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/epictetus/) — Detailed analysis of Epictetus's life, works, and philosophy
> - [Stanford Encyclopedia: Marcus Aurelius](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/marcus-aurelius/) — Scholarly treatment of Marcus Aurelius's *Meditations* and philosophical contributions
> 
> For further study, the surviving texts themselves remain essential: Epictetus's *Discourses* and *Enchiridion* (translated by Robin Hard or Robert Dobbin), Seneca's *Letters from a Stoic* and *Moral Essays*, and Marcus Aurelius's *Meditations* (Gregory Hays translation recommended for accessibility, Robin Hard for scholarly accuracy). For comprehensive treatment of Stoic physics and logic largely absent from Roman sources, consult A.A. Long and D.N. Sedley's *The Hellenistic Philosophers* (Cambridge, 1987), the standard scholarly collection of fragments and testimonia with commentary.

> [!cite] Reference
> **Metacognition and Journaling:**
> - [Developing Student Metacognition through Reflective Writing in an Upper Level Undergraduate Anatomy Course](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31965753/) - PubMed study on blog-based reflective writing
> - [Reflective journaling and metacognitive awareness: insights from a longitudinal study in higher education](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/14623943.2020.1716708) - Reflective Practice journal
> - [The Use of Reflective Journal as a Tool for Monitoring of Metacognition Growth in Writing](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/342133783) - ResearchGate publication
> - [Reflective Journal Writing as a Metacognitive Tool](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/266319213) - ResearchGate study
> - [Metacognition: ideas and insights from neuro- and educational sciences](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41539-021-00089-5) - *npj Science of Learning*
> - [The Self-Regulation-View in Writing-to-Learn: Using Journal Writing to Optimize Cognitive Load](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10648-020-09541-1) - *Educational Psychology Review*
> 
> **Memory Consolidation and Sleep:**
> - [Sleep—A brain-state serving systems memory consolidation](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0896627323002015) - *Neuron* journal
> - [System consolidation of memory during sleep](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3278619/) - PMC article
> - [The effects of bedtime writing on difficulty falling asleep](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29058942/) - PubMed polysomnography study
> - [About Sleep's Role in Memory](https://journals.physiology.org/doi/abs/10.1152/physrev.00032.2012) - *Physiological Reviews*
> - [Mechanisms of systems memory consolidation during sleep](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-019-0467-3) - *Nature Neuroscience*
> - [Memory and Sleep: How Sleep Cognition Can Change the Waking Mind](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7983127/) - PMC comprehensive review
> 
> **Stoic Philosophy and Marcus Aurelius:**
> - [Meditations - Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meditations) - Historical and philosophical overview
> - [Meditations by Marcus Aurelius: Book Summary, Key Lessons](https://dailystoic.com/meditations-marcus-aurelius/) - Daily Stoic analysis
> - [Marcus Aurelius (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/marcus-aurelius/) - Scholarly philosophical examination
> 
> **Behavioral Modification and Habit Formation:**
> - [The Power of Journaling for Positive Self-Change](https://www.thepaleomom.com/the-power-of-journaling-for-positive-self-change-self-monitoring-makes-all-the-difference/) - Self-monitoring research
> - [Self-Regulation Without Force: Can Awareness Leverage Habit Change](https://selfdeterminationtheory.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/01/2020_LudwigBrownBrewer_SelfRegulation.pdf) - SDT and awareness research
> - [From Habits to Self-Regulation: How Do We Change?](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3375665/) - PMC article on dual-process theory
> - [Understanding Learning Strategy Use Through the Lens of Habit](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10648-025-10087-3) - *Educational Psychology Review*
> - [Self-Efficacy in Habit Building](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8137900/) - PMC study on automaticity and self-efficacy

> [!cite] Reference
> The popular synthesis of Dweck's research program, reaching the broad audience and generating the cultural penetration of the growth mindset concept. Primary source for the conventional account in Phase I.

> [!cite] Reference
> The academic synthesis of Dweck's theoretical framework, providing the most rigorous statement of the implicit theories approach. Essential for understanding the technical distinctions between entity and incremental theories and their proposed effects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing attribution theory's three-dimensional model (locus, stability, controllability) and its predictions for persistence and emotion. The empirical foundation for Axiom 1 of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> Large-scale meta-analysis finding that the relationship between mindset and academic achievement is very small (r ≈ .10), and that mindset interventions produce near-zero effects in general populations with potentially meaningful effects only in at-risk populations. Critical evidence against Assumption 6.

> [!cite] Reference
> The largest preregistered mindset intervention study to date, reporting small but significant effects on challenge-seeking and GPA for lower-achieving students at under-resourced schools. Most favorable recent evidence for targeted mindset effects; used to establish the scope condition for intervention efficacy.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational paper establishing self-efficacy theory and the primacy of mastery experiences as the source of efficacy beliefs. The empirical and theoretical foundation for Axiom 2 of this report.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper establishing the learning/performance goal distinction and demonstrating that goal orientation mediates the relationship between ability beliefs and behavioral responses to failure. Critical foundation for Axiom 3.

> [!cite] Reference
> A systematic methodological critique finding that the evidence base for mindset theory's core causal claims is significantly weaker than typically presented, identifying issues with operationalization, causal inference, and publication bias. Essential methodological context for the assumption audit.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph. Establishes the theoretical framework, the contrast with rule-based theories, the empirical program, and the connection to model-theoretic semantics. Essential reading — but note that subsequent work has substantially revised the theory.

> [!cite] Reference
> The major empirical program. Systematic investigation of syllogistic, propositional, and relational reasoning from the mental models perspective. Contains the most complete presentation of the model-number predictions.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary theoretical alternative. Develops the mental logic account (PSYCOP) in detail. Essential for understanding what mental models theory is *contrasted against*; also contains important methodological critiques.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of the empirical literature. Remains a standard reference for the difficulty effects, belief-bias findings, and comparative performance of models and rules.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces pragmatic reasoning schemas — the main alternative that accommodates content effects within a rule-based framework. Directly challenges the interpretation of facilitation effects as evidence for mental models.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes the probabilistic rational analysis framework for the Wason selection task. Argues that human performance is near-optimal under realistic probabilistic assumptions — the most important challenge to both mental models and mental logic accounts.

> [!cite] Reference
> The canonical argument that apparent analog properties of mental imagery could be artifacts of tacit knowledge rather than evidence of a genuinely analog representational format. Applies directly to the analogical status of mental models.

> [!cite] Reference
> Develops the situation model framework in discourse comprehension — the closest empirical tradition to mental models theory with stronger methodological development and broader empirical coverage.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational empirical study demonstrating the undermining effect of external rewards on intrinsic motivation. Launched the SDT research programme by challenging behaviourist orthodoxy and establishing the importance of perceived locus of causality.

> [!cite] Reference
> The first comprehensive theoretical statement of SDT, integrating CET, OIT, and the basic needs framework. Establishes the organismic-integration philosophical architecture and the three-need structure.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive meta-analysis of the undermining effect, examining 128 studies. Finds that expected, tangible, contingent rewards undermine intrinsic motivation; unexpected, non-contingent, verbal rewards do not. Highly influential; also the subject of significant methodological debate (see Cameron & Pierce).

> [!cite] Reference
> The most-cited summary of SDT, accessible overview of the six mini-theories and key empirical findings. The best entry point for understanding the conventional SDT framework as intended by its architects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper distinguishing independent and interdependent self-construals and their implications for motivation, cognition, and affect. Essential context for evaluating SDT's cross-cultural universality claims, particularly regarding the autonomy need.

> [!cite] Reference
> Develops the distinction between need satisfaction and need frustration as partially independent dimensions — a significant internal revision of the original SDT model. Demonstrates that need frustration predicts ill-being beyond what need satisfaction alone explains.

> [!cite] Reference
> Proposes beneficence as a fourth candidate basic psychological need, independent of the three SDT needs, with empirical evidence for its contribution to well-being. Challenges the completeness claim of the three-need structure.

> [!cite] Reference
> Philosophical analysis of the concept of innateness and the evidential standards required to establish that a cognitive or motivational property is biologically grounded rather than universally acquired. Provides the philosophical framework for evaluating SDT's innateness claim rigorously.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal paper in the philosophy of agency distinguishing first-order desires from second-order volitions and locating freedom of will in the alignment between these levels. Provides the philosophical benchmark against which SDT's autonomy construct can be measured — and found to fall short of full self-determination.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text of attachment theory, establishing that affiliative bonds are a biological system that shapes developmental trajectories and internal working models. Provides the theoretical grounding for Axiom 4's claim that secure attachment is a precondition for autonomous motivation.

### From: [[stoicism-report-series-master|stoicism-report-series-master]]

### From: [[types-of-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|types-of-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text in which Peirce distinguishes the three modes of inference and articulates the logic of abduction for the first time. Essential primary reading for anyone seeking to understand the philosophical origins of the reasoning typology. Supports Phase II (Conceptual Foundations) and Phase III (Theoretical Landscape) throughout.

> [!cite] Reference
> Kahneman's synthesis of decades of research with Amos Tversky on cognitive heuristics and biases, organized around the Dual Process framework. Accessible and comprehensive treatment of how System 1 and System 2 shape everyday reasoning. Supports Phase III's discussion of dual process theory and Phase V's treatment of confirmation bias.

> [!cite] Reference
> A landmark developmental study of argumentative reasoning in children, adolescents, and adults. Kuhn demonstrates that the capacity to distinguish evidence from theory and evaluate argument quality develops slowly and unevenly. Essential for Phase III's discussion of developmental trajectories. Directly challenges over-optimistic assumptions about learners' natural reasoning capacity.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive meta-analysis of educational interventions ever conducted, synthesizing effect sizes across hundreds of studies. Relevant to Phase V for its findings on metacognitive strategies (d = 0.69), problem-based learning, and direct instruction. An essential reference for evidence-based claims about instructional effectiveness.

> [!cite] Reference
> Pivotal experimental study demonstrating that statistical (inductive) reasoning can transfer across domains under appropriate training conditions. Directly supports Phase III's discussion of the transfer debate and offers empirical grounds for cautious optimism about reasoning instruction. Supports Phase V's best-practice recommendations for scaffolded inductive learning.

> [!cite] Reference
> A provocative and influential argument that discovery-based learning is ineffective for novice learners, based on cognitive load theory. Challenges naive versions of inquiry-based pedagogy and supports Phase V's nuanced treatment of when inductive and direct instruction approaches are most appropriate. Essential for the limitations section.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that introduced the phrase "inference to the best explanation" as a philosophical term of art, making abductive inference central to the philosophy of science. Provides the philosophical grounding for the treatment of abduction in Phase II and throughout.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper on metacognition, introducing the concept as a technical term in psychology. Directly supports Phase II's definition of metacognition and Phase V's best-practice recommendations for metacognitive scaffolding. One of the most cited papers in educational psychology.

### From: [[vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28|vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary theoretical source for the received view under examination. Articulates the Input Hypothesis and its implications for vocabulary acquisition through comprehensible input. Essential for understanding the strongest form of the reading-primary position. Supports Phase I thesis articulation and the steel-man.

> [!cite] Reference
> Applies the Input Hypothesis directly to vocabulary acquisition through extensive reading, surveying the correlational and quasi-experimental research literature. Represents the reading-primary hypothesis at its most developed and accessible. Supports Phase I.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational reference for the empirical challenges to the received view: frequency threshold research, text coverage analysis, acquisition rate calculations, and the multi-dimensional model of word knowledge. Supports Phases III, IV, and the reconstructive synthesis. Essential.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original Matthew Effect paper — among the most influential articles in reading research. Provides the critical lens challenge and the evidence for bidirectional causality between reading and vocabulary. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original text coverage threshold study, establishing approximately 95% coverage as the level needed for adequate comprehension. Supports the empirical challenge in Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> The more refined threshold study establishing approximately 98% coverage for comfortable, acquisition-enabling independent reading. Supports Phase III empirical challenge.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational incidental acquisition rate study, estimating 5–15% probability of acquiring a usable meaning from a single contextual encounter. Both supports and conditionalises the received view. Supports Phase III.

> [!cite] Reference
> Meta-analysis confirming and refining the acquisition rate estimates from single contextual encounters. Supports Phase III empirical challenge.

> [!cite] Reference
> Review of the multiple-exposure research — documenting the 10–20+ encounters required for productive vocabulary knowledge. Supports Phase III and the multi-dimensionality challenge.

> [!cite] Reference
> The operational guide to the Three-Tier vocabulary framework and robust vocabulary instruction. Provides the practical alternative to reading-only vocabulary development and the evidence base for Tier 2 instruction effectiveness. Supports Phase IV and reconstruction.

> [!cite] Reference
> Longitudinal study demonstrating the causal role of early reading on later vocabulary, while also supporting the bidirectionality of the vocabulary-reading relationship. Supports Phase IV assumption challenge.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive framework for intentional vocabulary teaching within a frequency-indexed acquisition model. Supports the Channel A component of the reconstructive synthesis.

> [!cite] Reference
> Clarification and extension of the coverage threshold research with implications for vocabulary program design. Supports Phase V reconstruction.

### From: [[vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28|vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The standard comprehensive reference work in vocabulary acquisition research. Nation's framework for multidimensional word knowledge, coverage thresholds, and the design of vocabulary learning programs is presented in full here. Essential for any serious engagement with this literature. Particularly valuable: Chapter 2 (what is involved in knowing a word) and Chapter 4 (incidental learning through extensive reading).
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 2, 4, and 7 (vocabulary size goals).

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper articulating the self-amplifying vocabulary-comprehension dynamic. Stanovich's analysis of how early reading skill advantages compound over development remains the most influential single theoretical contribution to reading science. Readable and empirically grounded; a model of theoretical synthesis in educational psychology.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Full paper is relatively short (~48 pages); the vocabulary-comprehension spiral analysis in sections III–V is most directly relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph for Verbal Efficiency Theory. Perfetti presents the full theoretical argument and the supporting empirical research demonstrating the role of lexical access speed in determining comprehension quality.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 3 and 5 (verbal efficiency mechanisms and reading comprehension).

> [!cite] Reference
> The source of the most widely cited estimates of incidental vocabulary acquisition rate from reading. Nagy and Herman's analysis of the vocabulary-breadth-depth distinction and the case for extensive reading as the primary vocabulary-building mechanism remains central to the field.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Pages 19–35 in full; essential for understanding the quantitative case for reading-based acquisition.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text for the three-tier vocabulary model and its implications for instruction. Beck and colleagues make the case for intensive, multi-faceted instruction in Tier 2 academic vocabulary as a comprehension-support intervention. The practical protocols are directly applicable to deliberate vocabulary study programs.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapters 1–3 (the tier model and its rationale); Chapter 6 (instructional activities adaptable for self-directed study).

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational levels-of-processing paper. While not a vocabulary acquisition paper per se, its framework is the most important theoretical tool for understanding why some vocabulary learning strategies produce durable retention and others do not.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Full paper (13 pages); particularly the distinction between structural, phonemic, and semantic processing and the depth-retention relationship.

> [!cite] Reference
> The source of the empirical coverage threshold analysis (95–98%) and vocabulary size recommendations (8,000–9,000 word families for general text comprehension). Essential for understanding the quantitative case for vocabulary size as a reading bottleneck.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Pages 6–19 in full.

### From: [[walking-academic-reading-working-memory-deep-dive-2026-04-13|walking-academic-reading-working-memory-deep-dive-2026-04-13]]

### From: [[walking-and-academic-reading-socratic-exploration-2026-04-13|walking-and-academic-reading-socratic-exploration-2026-04-13]]

### From: [[zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23|zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive articulation of the three-phase cyclical model. Presents the sub-processes within each phase with considerable detail and connects them to the Social Cognitive Theory foundation. Essential reading for understanding the received view at its best. Particularly relevant for Phase I (thesis articulation) and for understanding the self-efficacy sub-processes in the Forethought Phase.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most comprehensive treatment of self-efficacy theory, including the four sources of self-efficacy information, the mechanisms of self-efficacy effects on performance, and the distinction between self-efficacy and related constructs. Foundational for understanding the model's motivational architecture. Relevant for Phase I and Phase IV (self-efficacy assumption analysis).

> [!cite] Reference
> Process-tracing study demonstrating that SRL training improved some regulatory processes but that monitoring-regulation decoupling persisted — students who monitored did not consistently regulate. Directly relevant to Phase III's empirical challenge. Important for understanding the conditions under which the monitoring-regulation link breaks down.

> [!cite] Reference
> Presents the Metacognitive and Affective Self-Regulated Learning (MASRL) model, which extends the Zimmerman framework to incorporate metacognitive experiences as the phenomenological signals mediating between monitoring and regulation. Directly relevant to Phase III and Phase IV's philosophical challenge: metacognitive experiences are shown to be influenced by fluency, affect, and task characteristics in ways that can decouple them from actual cognitive states.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational presentation of the COPES model, which serves as the primary alternative interpretation in Phase IV and as a key element of the reconstructive synthesis. The conditional, event-driven architecture provides the theoretical machinery for understanding *when* regulation is triggered, which the Zimmerman model underspecifies.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational articulation of the two-level metacognitive architecture (object level / meta level, monitoring / control). Provides the formal basis for understanding why monitoring-regulation decoupling is architecturally possible. Essential for Phase IV's alternative interpretations and for the calibration-dependency principle in Phase VI.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational text on single-loop vs. double-loop learning and defensive routines in organizations. Provides the structural analogue for the FAR Transfer phase (Phase VI) connecting the Zimmerman cycle's self-reflection dynamics to organizational learning failure modes.

> [!cite] Reference
> Zimmerman's own most sophisticated treatment of the monitoring-motivation intersection, acknowledging the role of metacognitive processes in mediating cyclical learning. Useful for nuanced engagement with the received view and for identifying where Zimmerman himself acknowledged conditional dependencies.

---

## Personal Knowledge Management

### From: [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15|goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph of goal-setting theory. Establishes the four mechanisms (direction, effort, persistence, strategy) and the specificity-difficulty relationship central to this report. Chapter 3 on moderators and Chapter 7 on self-set goals are most relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive review summarising 35 years of empirical evidence for goal-setting theory. Includes the most complete statement of the four mediating mechanisms and the boundary conditions for goal effects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The founding text of self-determination theory. Chapters on cognitive evaluation theory and organismic integration theory are essential background for understanding how goals interact with autonomy, competence, and relatedness needs.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most widely cited overview of SDT, presenting the autonomy-competence-relatedness framework and the internalisation continuum in accessible form. Section on autonomy-supportive environments is directly relevant to PKM design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper introducing the four-cell achievement goal framework that resolves earlier binary models. The mastery-approach cell is the most theoretically relevant to PKM, but the mastery-avoidance cell has underappreciated implications.

> [!cite] Reference
> The landmark study demonstrating that the *linguistic framing* of feedback (process vs. person praise) produces dramatically different motivational trajectories. Foundational evidence for this report's claim that goal language is causally operative.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates the interaction between goal contents (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) and context (autonomy-supportive vs. controlling), showing that intrinsic goals in autonomy-supportive contexts produce the strongest learning outcomes.

> [!cite] Reference
> The seminal review establishing that formative assessment produces substantial learning gains. Its framework of transparency, evidence, and gap-closing actions translates directly to PKM assessment-layer design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive statement of Zimmerman's tripartite SRL model. Essential for understanding how forethought, performance, and self-reflection phases interact and how goal-setting functions as a forethought activity.

> [!cite] Reference
> Integrates achievement goal theory with self-regulated learning theory. The 4×4 framework (phases × areas) introduced here is essential for understanding the full scope of regulatory activity in PKM.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper on the monitoring-control framework of metacognition. The meta-level/object-level architecture is the basis for understanding how self-assessment operates in learning contexts.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper that named metacognition as a field and proposed the person-task-strategy taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge. Foundational vocabulary for all subsequent metacognitive research.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational monograph of goal-setting theory. Establishes the four mechanisms (direction, effort, persistence, strategy) and the specificity-difficulty relationship central to this report. Chapter 3 on moderators and Chapter 7 on self-set goals are most relevant.

### From: [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05|pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper of Cognitive Load Theory. Demonstrates that means-ends analysis during problem-solving saturates working memory in ways that inhibit schema formation, while worked-example study produces superior transfer. Essential background for understanding why CLT's core claim (minimise extraneous load) is counter-intuitive to common instructional practice.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary systematic articulation of the three-type CLT framework and its instructional design applications. Introduces germane load as a distinct category and develops the design principles — worked examples, split-attention and redundancy effects, completion tasks — that constitute CLT's practical toolkit. The reference most directly underlying this report's theoretical framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces the episodic buffer as the fourth component of Baddeley's working memory model — the multi-modal integration system relevant to synthesis tasks. Particularly important for this report's claim that the episodic buffer is the target cognitive space for PKM, as it is the system that integrates information from multiple sources into coherent meaning.

> [!cite] Reference
> Comprehensive review of the expertise-reversal effect and its implications for adaptive instruction. Provides the theoretical basis for Section 8's developmental model of PKM design and the claim that optimal PKM system design is expertise-relative, not universal.

> [!cite] Reference
> Introduces the concept of "desirable difficulties" — instructional conditions that appear to impede performance during training but enhance long-term retention and transfer. Essential theoretical background for Section 7's argument that effortful, generative PKM activity outperforms smooth, fluent capture.

> [!cite] Reference
> Systematic review of the cognitive offloading research programme. Establishes the conditions under which external storage genuinely reduces cognitive load (lower accessing cost than internal reconstruction) and identifies the adaptive vs. maladaptive offloading distinction — directly relevant to Section 4's analysis of when PKM functions as cognitive extension vs. cognitive overhead.

> [!cite] Reference
> Demonstrates that a single retrieval practice test produces better long-term retention outcomes than four re-studying sessions. The empirical basis for Section 7's recommendations around retrieval practice, write-from-memory protocols, and active recall over passive review in PKM.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes that the depth of cognitive processing during encoding, not the amount of repetition, determines memory quality. The theoretical foundation for Section 7's critique of shallow note-capture practices and the argument for elaborative encoding.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary contemporary reference for Zettelkasten methodology as adapted for academic and non-fiction knowledge work. Provides the systematic articulation of Luhmann's slip-box practice that Section 9 evaluates through a CLT lens.

> [!cite] Reference
> The primary contemporary reference for both PARA and Progressive Summarization methodologies. Provides the systematic descriptions of both methods that Section 9 evaluates through a CLT lens.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational philosophical argument that cognitive processes can extend beyond the boundaries of skull-and-skin — directly relevant to the claim that well-designed PKM systems can function as genuine cognitive extensions. Provides the theoretical foundation for the cognitive-offloading analysis in Section 4.

> [!cite] Reference
> The 20-year retrospective and theoretical revision of the 1998 CLT framework. Includes the contested revision of germane load's status (from separate category to productive component of intrinsic load) and addresses the expertise-reversal evidence in the context of adaptive instruction design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper of Cognitive Load Theory. Demonstrates that means-ends analysis during problem-solving saturates working memory in ways that inhibit schema formation, while worked-example study produces superior transfer. ## Personal Knowledge Management

### From: [[cog-sci-pkm-the-role-of-reading-in-pkm|cog-sci-pkm-the-role-of-reading-in-pkm]]

> [!cite] Reference
> **Primary Theoretical Sources**
> 
> Anderson, R. C., & Pearson, P. D. (1984). A schema-theoretic view of basic processes in reading comprehension. In P. D. Pearson (Ed.), *Handbook of Reading Research* (pp. 255–291). Longman.
> 
> Ahrens, S. (2017). *How to Take Smart Notes: One Simple Technique to Boost Writing, Learning and Thinking.* CreateSpace.
> 
> Bartlett, F. C. (1932). *Remembering: A Study in Experimental and Social Psychology.* Cambridge University Press.
> 
> Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about Knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.
> 
> Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed practice in verbal recall tasks: A review and quantitative synthesis. *Psychological Bulletin, 132*(3), 354–380.
> 
> Craik, F. I. M., & Lockhart, R. S. (1972). Levels of processing: A framework for memory research. *Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior, 11*(6), 671–684.
> 
> Dunlosky, J., Rawson, K. A., Marsh, E. J., Nathan, M. J., & Willingham, D. T. (2013). Improving students' learning with effective learning techniques. *Psychological Science in the Public Interest, 14*(1), 4–58.
> 
> Ebbinghaus, H. (1885). *Über das Gedächtnis: Untersuchungen zur experimentellen Psychologie.* Duncker & Humblot. [Translated as *Memory: A Contribution to Experimental Psychology*, 1913]
> 
> Forte, T. (2022). *Building a Second Brain: A Proven Method to Organize Your Digital Life and Unlock Your Creative Potential.* Atria Books.

## Reading Comprehension

### From: [[situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29|situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive presentation of the Construction-Integration model, providing the computational framework for understanding how three levels of text representation are built. Phases II and IV of this report draw heavily on this source.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The section on situation models (pp. 170–175) is the most directly relevant to this report's analytical framework.
> 
> **Historical Significance:** This paper transformed reading comprehension research from a description-level field into a mechanistically grounded computational science.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational book-length treatment of discourse comprehension strategy, introducing the textbase/situation model distinction in its fully developed form and providing extensive evidence for the three-level architecture.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** Chapter 2 (propositional representation), Chapter 5 (situation models), and Chapter 11 (individual differences in comprehension strategy).

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive review of Event Indexing Model research, synthesizing experimental evidence for the five CATSM dimensions. Central to the Phase II framework and the Causal Spine Bottleneck analysis in Phase IV.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The section on causal dimension tracking (pp. 171–175) is directly relevant to the Causal Spine Bottleneck analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The landmark study establishing the self-explanation effect. Provides the primary empirical basis for the Generation Constraint argument in Phases III and IV.
> 
> **Recommended Sections:** The analysis of "good" versus "poor" self-explanations (pp. 163–172) is particularly relevant.

> [!cite] Reference
> The influential study documenting that retrieval practice (free recall) produces substantially better learning than re-reading, providing the evidence for the Fluency Illusion analysis and the post-reading recall intervention.

> [!cite] Reference
> A comprehensive review and theoretical synthesis of inference generation research during reading. Central to the Generation Deficit analysis in Phase IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> Documents the interaction between prior knowledge and text coherence in situation model construction, providing evidence for the prior knowledge bootstrapping analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The baseball knowledge study demonstrating that domain knowledge overrides reading ability in predicting comprehension performance. Essential evidence for the prior knowledge dimension of situation model construction.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original generation effect study. Foundational to the generation constraint analysis.

> [!cite] Reference
> The original advance organizer study. Provides the empirical basis for the pre-reading frame protocol.

## Self Regulated Learning

### From: [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24-1|srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]]

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational chapter establishing the three-phase cyclical SRL model with the detailed sub-components of each phase. The primary theoretical source for the Forethought and Self-Reflection Phase architectures analyzed in this report. Essential reading for Phases II and IV.

> [!cite] Reference
> The most accessible summary of Zimmerman's model, translating the theoretical framework into practical implications for educational contexts. Supports Phases II, V, and the appendix tools.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive statement of self-efficacy theory, including the four-source model and the calibration/accuracy distinction that is central to the mechanism analysis in Phase IV. Recommended for the self-efficacy sections of Phase IV specifically.

> [!cite] Reference
> The foundational paper establishing the three-dimensional attribution framework (locus, stability, controllability) and its motivational consequences. Central to Phase IV's attribution mechanism analysis and the Self-Reflection Phase tool design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The paper establishing the mastery/helpless response pattern distinction and its connection to goal orientation and implicit theories of intelligence. Central to the adaptive/defensive inference distinction and the language architecture principles in Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Establishes the mastery-referenced evaluation standard as the key variable producing adaptive motivational orientations. Supports the self-evaluation mechanism analysis in Phase IV and the language architecture in Phase V.

> [!cite] Reference
> Meta-analytic synthesis of feedback research establishing the three levels of effective formative feedback (task, process, self-regulation). Directly supports the language architecture principles in Phase V. Recommended for the practical tools section.

> [!cite] Reference
> The definitive review of goal-setting theory research establishing specificity, difficulty, and proximity as the key goal features. Supports the goal-setting mechanism analysis in Phase IV and the three-part learning goal framework.

> [!cite] Reference
> Foundational review establishing formative assessment practices as among the most powerful interventions in educational research. Supports the language architecture principles in Phase V and the self-evaluation component of the Self-Reflection Phase design.

> [!cite] Reference
> The theoretical architecture of SDT, including the autonomy-support principles underlying the motivational language framework in Phase V. Essential for understanding why controlling self-talk is motivationally hazardous.

> [!cite] Reference
> Empirical research demonstrating the specific effects of goal specificity and progress feedback on self-efficacy and achievement in academic writing contexts. Supports Phase III's evidence review of goal-setting effects.

> [!cite] Reference
> The "wise interventions" framework providing an evidence-based account of how brief, targeted attributional and mindset interventions produce durable behavioral change. Supports the attribution retraining dimension of Self-Reflection Phase tool design.
